UNITARY COBOUNDARY WEAK QUASI-HOPF ALGEBRAS IN THE
KAZHDAN-LUSZTIG-FINKELBERG THEOREM
SERGIO CIAMPRONE, MARCO VALERIO GIANNONE, AND CLAUDIA PINZARI
Dedicated to the memory of Sergio Doplicher and John E. Roberts.
Abstract. We construct, for every complex simple Lie algebra g and every positive
integer level k, a unitary modular tensor category structure on the module category of
the affine vertex operator algebra Vgk, equivalent to the quantum group fusion category
C(g, q, ℓ), within the Doplicher–Roberts quantum group duality program for the Wess–
Zumino–Witten model. The construction reaches positive integer levels that Finkelberg’s
argument does not cover, namely E6 and E7 at k = 1 and E8 at k = 2, and it treats simplylaced and non-simply-laced types uniformly, whereas the Kazhdan–Lusztig construction
was carried out for the simply-laced case.
We establish that a weak quasi-fibre structure on a functor preserving a coboundary
symmetry in the sense of Drinfeld extends a symmetric functor under permutation symmetry. Using Wenzl’s functor associated with the unitary quantum group fusion category,
we construct such a structure compatible with the associativity morphisms, yielding unitary coboundary weak Hopf C
∗
-algebras for all Lie types and positive integer levels, a
new class axiomatically introduced here. Via a specialized Drinfeld twist for our algebras
and the Wenzl de-quantization curve, this structure is transported onto the Zhu algebra,
which becomes a unitary coboundary weak quasi-Hopf C
∗
-algebra with a 3-coboundary
associator. Rigidity is obtained from the quantum group side, without the Knizhnik–
Zamolodchikov equations or the Verlinde formula.
We further analyze the type A case via classification methods based on Kazhdan–Wenzl
theory and our weak Hopf algebra framework, which gives insight into the determination
of associativity from the braiding in the general case. Our methods settle several further
problems: Galindo’s question on the uniqueness of unitary tensor structures, Kirillov’s
conjecture on the positivity of a Hermitian form on the braided tensor category of modules
of an affine Lie algebra introduced by Beilinson, Feigin and Mazur, the quantum group
structure on the Zhu algebra sought by Frenkel and Zhu, and the Kazhdan–Lusztig–
Finkelberg equivalence for all Lie types and all positive integer levels without passing
through the negative shifted levels, as raised by Huang. We also compare our braided
tensor structure with that of Huang and Lepowsky.

1. Introduction
1.1. Historical context of the KLF equivalence. Let g be a f.d. complex simple Lie
algebra and k a positive integer, defined as the physical level. Let d denote the ratio of
the squared lengths of the long to the short roots (d = 1 for simply laced types, d = 2 for

UNITARY COBOUNDARY WEAK QUASI-HOPF ALGEBRAS 3
types B, C, F, and d = 3 for G2). We define the shifted level as ℓ = d(k + hˇ), where hˇ is
the dual Coxeter number of g.
The current algebra of what is now known as the Wess–Zumino-Witten (WZW) model
was shown by Witten [187] to satisfy the commutation relations of affine Kac-Moody algebras at positive integer levels. Knizhnik and Zamolodchikov showed that the n-point
correlation functions of primary fields of the WZW model satisfy a system of partial differential equations, the Knizhnik–Zamolodchikov (KZ) equations [114].
Pioneering work by Kohno [116] established that the braid group representations with
respect to the vector representation of a non-exceptional Lie algebra, arising from the
monodromy of the KZ equations in his framework, are equivalent to those originating from
quantum groups. Kohno initially constructed these integrable connections on the full tensor
product of finite-dimensional representations of the Lie algebra g under a generic parameter
λ. In the particular case of sln, he found the associated Hecke algebra representations.
While he noted that the KZ equations appearing in conformal field theory were related
to his framework when the parameter specializes to λ = 1/ℓ, he restricted his explicit
implementation of the affine framework at a positive integer level strictly to the case of the
affine Kac–Moody algebra slb2, drawing upon the operator formalism of Tsuchiya and Kanie
[170]. By utilizing Wenzl’s classification of unitarizable Hecke algebra representations
at roots of unity, Kohno established the unitarizability of these geometric braid group
representations for both the vector and higher irreducible representations of sl2.
Crucially, this framework relied fundamentally on the multiplicity-free nature of the
sl2 fusion rules and the direct path-algebra descriptions of the corresponding Temperley–
Lieb algebra. For higher-rank Lie algebras, the emergence of non-trivial multiplicities and
higher-dimensional intertwining spaces introduces deep combinatorial obstructions.
Inspired by these results, Drinfeld [47, 48] introduced a deep braided tensor category
structure on the category of finite-dimensional g-modules by equipping it with an associator explicitly derived from the KZ equations. He proved the equivalence at a categorical
level between his category—known as the Drinfeld category—and the rigid braided tensor
category of modules over the formal quantum group Uh(g), marking a foundational breakthrough in representation theory. Drinfeld obtained rigidity of the Drinfeld category by
constructing an antipode on the associated quasi-bialgebra, following the Drinfeld–Kohno
theorem, via an operation that he called a twist of a quantum group [47].
Subsequently, inspired by Drindeld’s results, Kazhdan and Lusztig [109] extended Kohno’s
braid group equivalence to the affine setting at negative rational shifted levels, constructing a braided tensor category structure on certain non-semisimple module categories O−ℓ
over the corresponding affine Kac-Moody algebras (specifically for simply laced types).
They proved a categorical equivalence between O−ℓ and the representation categories of
corresponding quantum groups at the root of unity q = e
iπ
ℓ . A main result of their work
is a direct proof of rigidity of O−ℓ.
For the positive integer levels, Finkelberg followed the algebraic geometric approach developed by Beilinson, Feigin, and Mazur [11] for the semisimple braided tensor category O˜
ℓ
associated to an affine Lie algebra, and constructed a functor to a semisimple subquotient

4 S. CIAMPRONE, M.V. GIANNONE, AND C. PINZARI
fusion category O˜
−ℓ of a Kazhdan-Lusztig category at the opposite shifted level [55], and
thus, by the Kazhdan-Lusztig equivalence, to a semisimple subquotient fusion category
C(g, q, ℓ) of the corresponding quantum group at a root of unity.
Frenkel and Zhu established a vertex operator algebra structure on a certain representation Lk,0 of the affine Kac-Moody—and analogously for the Virasoro algebra—in such a
way that the representations of Lk,0 give rise to the WZW model [61]. We denote by Vgk
this vertex operator algebra.
Following an analytic approach based on differential equations, Huang and Lepowsky
constructed a braided tensor category structure on the module category of a vertex operator
algebra in a very general setting, including the affine cases, for which they relied on the KZ
equations [85]–[88]. Huang gave a rigorous proof of the Verlinde formula and the fusion
rules [178] and showed its relevance to prove rigidity and modularity [92], [93].
To complete the proof of the tensor equivalence of his functor O˜
ℓ → O˜−ℓ
, Finkelberg
needed the fusion rules and the Verlinde formula as well [56], established in his setting by
Teleman and Faltings [53], [171], guaranteeing that the K-rings of the positive and negative
shifted level categories are based-isomorphic. He also relied on rigidity of Kazhdan-Lusztig
category O−ℓ and of the category of finite-dimensional representations of the simple Lie
algebra g. He also derived rigidity of O˜
ℓ
from his tensor equivalence.
Summarizing, the existing proof of the tensor equivalence O˜
ℓ → C(g, q, ℓ) relies on results spanning distinct algebraic and geometric settings across both positive integer and
negative shifted levels. It requires separate quotient constructions to yield the semisimple
subquotients where rigidity defines the necessary tensor ideals, and uses rigidity to obtain
tensoriality of the equivalence. Furthermore, because the Kazhdan–Lusztig construction
does not apply to certain negative rational shifted levels, Finkelberg’s equivalence, and
consequently the proof of rigidity of O˜
ℓ
, do not cover some exceptional cases—namely, E6
and E7 at level k = 1, and E8 at levels k = 1, 2. Additionally, it is not easy to find literature explicitly extending the Kazhdan–Lusztig rigid braided tensor category construction
to non-simply laced Lie algebras, due to the greater complexities of the KZ equations.
Regarding the non-simply laced types, it is noted in Sect. 2.6 of [55] and Sect. 2, part (f)
of [56] that due to a uniform lower bound constraint of ℓ needed to guarantee rigidity of
O−ℓ, the rigidity of O˜
ℓ and the tensor equivalence property of the Finkelberg functor may
be unproved for low levels k ≤ 2, requiring further case-by-case study.
Taken together, the existing proof of the equivalence is distributed over [109], [55], [56],
[53] and [171]. Kazhdan and Lusztig carried out their construction for the simply-laced
types, while Finkelberg’s functor is stated for the other types as well.
Consequently, the need to describe a direct connection between affine Lie algebra module categories at positive integer levels and quantum groups emerged, see e.g. Gannon’s
observation in Sect. 6.2.3 of [68]. Notably, in [97], Huang explained in detail the history
of Finkelberg’s theorem and the need of finding a direct proof—one that bypasses the obstructions arising from the negative shifted levels and includes the exceptional cases (see
Problem 5). The interest of a direct construction extends beyond the case at hand. There
are conjectural equivalences between braided tensor categories of modules for other vertex

UNITARY COBOUNDARY WEAK QUASI-HOPF ALGEBRAS 5
operator algebras and categories of modules for quantum groups for which no Kazhdan–
Lusztig category is available; in those cases the route through negative shifted levels is
unavailable. Huang has pointed out to us that this is his main reason of interest in the
problem. A direct construction of Finkelberg’s equivalence is therefore a natural starting
point for those cases.
In this spirit, we start with the quantum group fusion category. Wenzl showed in [185]
that this category can be constructed intrinsically for all Lie types and all positive integer
levels, treating the simply-laced and non-simply-laced cases uniformly, for the roots of
unity of the kind q = e
iπ/ℓ starting with a fundamental (generating) representation V of
Uq(g), see Theorem 5.4 in [25] for an explicit proof.
Starting from Wenzl’s realization of C(g, q, ℓ) and adopting a direct, operator-algebraic
framework, we give a direct proof of the equivalence with a category of modules of the
corresponding affine vertex operator algebra, in a single self-contained text. In this way
we circumvent the Verlinde formula dependency of the equivalence with the categories
of modules of affine vertex operator algebras (or affine Lie algebras), the passage to the
negative integer levels, and we derive rigidity of these categories from the equivalence,
thereby also rendering boundary cases uniformly accessible. We describe our approach in
the next subsection, state the problems solved in this paper in subsection 1.3, and describe
the main results in section 2. In section 3 we describe the organization of the rest of the
paper.
1.2. Overview of our approach. In the operator algebraic approach to quantum field
theory (AQFT) introduced by Haag and Kastler [80], the subsequent work of Doplicher,
Haag, and Roberts established the rigidity of the associated symmetric tensor C
∗
-categories
of superselection sectors from physically motivated principles, yielding Dirac’s particleantiparticle duality as a structural consequence [38], [39], [40], [81].
For high-dimensional theories, a deep result by Doplicher and Roberts established that
the category of localizable charges is equivalent to the representation category of a unique
compact group having the role of a global gauge group [41]. The Doplicher–Roberts compact group associated with the category describes rigidity via the passage to the conjugate
representation of the group. A closely related result was independently obtained by Deligne
for affine group schemes over a field [30].
In low-dimensional theories, one encounters unitary rigid braided tensor C
∗
-categories,
where the braided symmetry is given by unitary representations of the braid group acting
on tensor powers of an object [57, 58].
Parallel to this, as recalled in Subsect. 1.1,, Jones index theory led to the construction
of rigid tensor C
∗
-categories from inclusions of von Neumann algebras with trivial center
(subfactors) and yielded unitary representations of the braid group satisfying Temperley–
Lieb relations; conversely, inclusions of subfactors arise from such relations [100].
Shortly after Vaughan Jones’ discovery of subfactor theory and the Jones polynomial,
Wenzl connected the representation theory of Hecke algebras Hn(q) of type A at roots of
unity to inclusions of finite-index II1 subfactors. This breakthrough provided a foundational framework for the unitary structures linking subfactor theory, quantum groups, and

6 S. CIAMPRONE, M.V. GIANNONE, AND C. PINZARI
two-dimensional conformal field theory [182, 183]. Wenzl explicitly constructed and classified finite-dimensional, irreducible representations of the non-semisimple algebras Hn(q)
that are unitarizable, thereby constructing new hyperfinite II1 subfactors that generalize
the Temperley-Lieb-Jones constructions. As observed in [183], via quantum Schur-Weyl
duality, these representations of Hecke algebras can naturally be viewed as arising from
the endomorphism algebras of tensor powers of the fundamental representation of Uq(slN )
[99].
Drinfeld introduced quantum groups at the ICM in Berkeley in 1986 [45]. Quantum
groups provide rigidity via the antipode. The representation theory of quantum groups at
roots of unity was developed by Lusztig [119, 122], Reshetikhin, Turaev [155].
We are naturally led to ask whether rigidity of categories on the conformal field theory side, and in particular for Vgk
, can be explained by an equivalence with a category
of representations of a semisimple quantum gauge group in the sense of the DoplicherRoberts program. We therefore look for a natural construction of a quantum version of
the Doplicher–Roberts compact group for braided categories. A major difference with
Drinfeld setting is that, in our setting, the involved braided tensor categories, and especially the quantum group fusion category, are not given from the start with a fibre functor
to the category of vector spaces.
Mack and Schomerus pioneered the question of bridging the Doplicher–Roberts program on global quantum gauge groups and field algebras in conformal field theory with
Drinfeld’s work on the Drinfeld-Kohno theorem mentioned in Subsect. 1.1. Specifically,
they introduced the weak quasi-Hopf algebras that extend Drinfeld’s original structures,
implementing them for the sl2-case as a quantum analogue of Doplicher–Roberts compact
groups [124, 165].
Their early construction of both the weak quasi-Hopf algebras and the analogue of
the Doplicher–Roberts field algebra encountered complications, arising primarily from the
non-triviality of the associator and a lack of uniqueness or naturality beyond the sl2 case.
Wenzl extended the construction of subfactors from quantum groups at roots of unity
and constructed unitary braided tensor structures on the quantum group fusion category
uniformly for all Lie types, in [184], [185].
Mack and Schomerus program was not developed further until recently, when two of us
found a natural construction of Mack-Schomerus weak quasi-Hopf algebras associated to
the quantum group fusion category of Uq(slN ) for all positive integer levels [25].
Our first aim in this paper is to put the examples of [25] into a new theory of weak Hopf
algebras, and then extend the construction to all other Lie types.
Specifically, we adopt an operator-algebraic approach where unitarity and C
∗
-structures
with unitary representations of the braid group play a primary role.
Our approach heavily relies on the foundational C
∗
-algebraic techniques for quantum
group fusion categories developed by Wenzl. We consider Wenzl linear functor
W : C(g, q, ℓ) → Hilb
and construct on it a weak tensor structure in the sense of Definition 4.8.

UNITARY COBOUNDARY WEAK QUASI-HOPF ALGEBRAS 7
We achieve new examples AW (g, q, ℓ) in this paper for all Lie types and levels, with
different methods as compared to the type A case treated in [25], without using the fusion
rules, see Theorem 2.5. For g = slN we recover our earlier examples. We prove that these
algebras satisfy properties of compatible unitary coboundary weak Hopf algebras in a sense
that we introduce in Definition 30.1.
The class of unitary coboundary weak (quasi-)Hopf algebras (Definition 28.1) admits a
categorical characterization that significantly simplifies their axiomatic description; see
Theorem 29.1. In the specific case of a symmetric tensor C
∗
-category endowed with a unitary fibre functor to the category of Hilbert spaces, this characterization reduces precisely
to the requirement that the functor is symmetric. This constraint is required and, in fact,
plays a fundamental role in the Doplicher–Roberts duality theory for compact groups, in
determining the fibre functor uniquely.
In the braided setting, the categorical characterization of a unitary coboundary weak
quasi-Hopf algebra requires finding a weak quasi-tensor structure on the given functor
implemented by coisometric fibre maps Fρ,σ associated with the tensor product bifunctor of the underlying braided category. It is required that the structure maps respect
the coboundary symmetries of both the source and target categories. Indeed, the unique
coboundary symmetry on the category of Hilbert spaces is the standard permutation symmetry. Since the coboundary symmetry of a braided tensor category is obtained by multiplying the braiding by a 2-coboundary derived from a square root of the ribbon element,
this characterization forces a rigid constraint: via the fibre functor, the braided symmetry of the category is uniquely determined as the permutation symmetry modified by this
square root 2-coboundary.
Formulas of this nature indeed govern the explicit structure of the braided symmetry in
categories arising from conformal field theory, such as loop group conformal nets or affine
vertex operator algebras (see Remarks 29.2, 38.16, and references therein). This operator
algebraically realizes the categorical balancing relation where the double braiding equals
the 2-coboundary defined by the twist. On an irreducible component of the minimum
energy subspace of the tensor product, the braiding evaluates precisely to the square root
of the Virasoro phases multiplied by the permutation symmetry, see Theorem 4.1 in [93].
While the braided symmetry in these settings is traditionally derived from the monodromy of the Knizhnik–Zamolodchikov equations, our framework provides an independent
method for constructing this symmetry entirely via unitary coboundary weak quasi-Hopf
algebras with coisometric structure maps. In our view, this construction beautifully realizes an insightful perspective of Vaughan Jones on the profound relevance of manifest
unitarity in conformal field theory (cf. [150]).
In the quantum group setting, the action of the R-matrix on tensor product spaces does
not take an analogous simple diagonal form. Consequently, the associated coboundary
matrix does not act trivially. Since this matrix induces the unitary structure on the fusion
spaces [185], this unitary structure effectively measures the deviation of the R-matrix
from acting strictly via the square roots of the ribbon phases–a purely quantum effect.
We explicitly quantify this phenomenon. Specifically, we show that the unitary modular
quantum group fusion category C(g, q, ℓ), which is described by the unitary representations

8 S. CIAMPRONE, M.V. GIANNONE, AND C. PINZARI
of our unitary coboundary weak Hopf algebra AW (g, q, ℓ) by Theorem 2.5, is fundamentally
incompatible with the existence of coisometric fusion projections due to the weakly trivial
associator of AW (g, q, ℓ) and the non-integrality of the quantum dimensions of C(g, q, ℓ) (see
Theorem 16.8). Thus, extracting coisometric fusion projections via polar decomposition
inevitably twists the weak Hopf algebra into a genuine weak quasi-Hopf algebra, necessarily
equipped with a 3-coboundary associator.
By applying an analytic Drinfeld twist method and Wenzl’s de-quantization curve to the
weak Hopf algebras AW (g, q, ℓ), we obtain a self-contained construction of a unitary rigid
braided tensor structure on the module category of the affine vertex operator algebra Vgk
(see Corollary 2.9).
A pivotal feature of our unitary structure on Rep(Vgk
) and analytic twist is that it equips
Zhu’s minimum energy functor with a canonical weak quasi-tensor structure (F0, G0), in
a way that F0 coincides with the restriction of Huang-Lepowsky tensor product structure
map, and moreover the twist forces F0 to be coisometric and G0 to be the isometric Hilbert
space adjoint of F0, a property we establish in Theorem 21.3. Comparing with HuangLepowsky ribbon braided tensor structure, at this stage we have established an equivalence
of the two categories, the ribbon structures and the tensor product bifunctors for the affine
vertex operator alegbras at positive integer levels.
In searching the Drinfeld twist we have been inspired by the categorical framework of
Drinfeld-Kohno theorem established by Neshveyev and Tuset for compact quantum groups
[139]. However, our twist seems different from their twist. Our twist may be regarded as a
specialization at the root of unity to the fusion algebra AW (g, q, ℓ) of the original Drinfeld
twist in the formal setting, arising from a square root of Drinfeld coboundary matrix [47],
that we consider in an operator algebraic sense.
We prove all the properties of the affine vertex operator algebra fusion category at a
positive integer level using the methods of quantum symmetry and the Drinfeld twist.
Specifically, the ribbon-braided tensor structure, fusion rules, and rigidity of the unitary
quantum group fusion category C(g, q, ℓ) induce a corresponding structure on the weak Hopf
C
∗
-algebra AW (g, q, ℓ). From this position, we transfer the structure to the Zhu algebra
via our Drinfeld twist and the de-quantization curve used in [185] to prove unitarity of the
fusion category.
Wenzl’s curve gives an algebra isomorphism between AW (g, q, ℓ) and the Zhu algebra
A(Vgk). The twist transports the rest of the structure: coproduct, (weak trivial) associator,
antipode, R-matrix, unitary structure, making the Zhu algebra into a unitary coboundary
weak quasi-Hopf C
∗
-algebra with 3-coboundary associator. From this position, we lift the
structure to the module category Rep(Vgk) of the vertex operator algebra via Zhu’s linear
equivalence and its inverse. In this way, we obtain the structure of a unitary rigid ribbonbraided tensor category on Rep(Vgk
) braided tensor equivalent to the quantum group fusion
category.
We deduce the rigidity of our VOA fusion category Rep(Vgk) directly from our tensor
equivalence, driven by the antipode of AW (g, q, ℓ) and our specialized Drinfeld twist.
This approach allows us to bypass the need to dualize through negative levels, provide an explicit, self-contained proof of the Kazhdan-Luszting-Finkelberg theorem that

UNITARY COBOUNDARY WEAK QUASI-HOPF ALGEBRAS 9
includes the exceptional boundary cases, and simultaneously settle the problem of Galindo
on uniqueness of the unitary structure in a tensor category, as well as those of Frenkel-Zhu
and Doplicher-Roberts for the WZW model within the framework of vertex operator algebras. Along our path, we solve Kirillov’s conjecture in the algebraic-geometric framework
of Beilinson, Feigin, and Mazur utilized by Finkelberg, using general Tannakian methods
for weak quasi-Hopf algebras, Finkelberg’s equivalence theorem, and Wenzl’s unitarity of
C(g, q, ℓ).
We complete the comparison between our unitary ribbon braided structure with the
Huang–Lepowsky structure. We find a complete identification of the full categorical data
(ribbon structure, tensor product bifunctor, braiding, and associativity morphisms) for the
classical Lie types and G2. For all Lie types, we obtain identifications between the two
structures for the braiding and associativity morphisms on many objects involving tensor
products with the fundamental representations, see Theorem 2.11. To keep the focus on the
core ideas developed in this paper, the detailed proofs of Theorems 8.13 and 8.21, as well
as their applicability to the stated Lie types, are deferred to our companion paper [149].
In this way we obtain an independent construction of the Huang-Lepowsky braided tensor
structure on Rep(Vgk
) which does not depend on the Knizhnik-Zamolodchikov equations,
and a proof of rigidity of this category that does not depend on the Verlinde formula or
fusion rules.
Finally, in [150], we provide a conceptual and expository historical overview of the
main ideas of our proof of the KLF theorem and its applications to the unitarizability of
categories arising from conformal field theory, and we outline directions for future research.
The remainder of the paper is organized as follows. In the subsequent Subsect. 1.3, we
we state the open Problems 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, and 6 which are of primary interest in our paper
(with Problem 1 serving as our original motivation). In Sect. 2, we state our main results.
In Sect. 3, we provide an outline of the rest of the paper. In doing so, we discuss the
utility of the KLF theorem (or other instances of equivalences coming, e.g., from pointed
fusion categories) to transport unitary structures from the quantum group fusion category
to the affine vertex operator algebra fusion category.
1.3. Statements of the problems under consideration in this paper. As outlined
in the previous subsections, our uniform operator-algebraic framework is connected to
several long-standing problems across algebraic quantum field theory (AQFT), conformal
field theory, and tensor category theory. For clarity, we state these problems here.
As recalled in Subsect. 1.2, the Doplicher–Roberts theorem characterizes the category
of high-dimensional localizable charges by the representation category of a unique compact
group. However, in low dimensions, the statistics dimension may be non-integer, meaning
the category cannot correspond to a standard compact group. Because the braid group
naturally appears in low-dimensional quantum field theories, Doplicher and Roberts posed
the following problem, which served as the original motivation for this paper:
Problem 1. (Sect. 7 in [43]) Can we extend the duality theory for compact groups [41]
Theorem 6.1 to more general objects, by replacing strict symmetric monoidal C*-categories

10 S. CIAMPRONE, M.V. GIANNONE, AND C. PINZARI
by strict braided monoidal C*-categories? What is the full class of compact group-like
objects which arise in this way?
Parallel to this, in the study of conformal field theory (CFT), Moore and Seiberg conjectured analytic properties of quantum fields and discovered polynomial equations that
later evolved into the notion of modular tensor categories [129, 130, 154, 155, 173]. Noting
that in various examples of CFT, the braid matrix is closely related to the R-matrix of
specific quantum groups, they posed the following:
Problem 2. (Sect. 9 in [130]) Understand chiral algebras of conformal field theory as
generalization of quantum groups.
Regarding construction of unitary structures on modular fusion categories, Kirillov defined hermitian forms on the quantum group fusion category C(g, q, ℓ) in [111] and on the
Beilinson-Feigin-Mazur category O˜
ℓ at positive integer levels in [113], and conjectured that
they are unitary. The quantum group case was settled by Wenzl in [185], while the affine
Lie algebra case, to the authors’ knowledge, remained open.
Problem 3. (Remark 10.7 in [113]) The natural conjecture, parallel to the quantum group
case, is that so defined inner product on the spaces of morphisms is positive definite. So
far, we have no proof of it except for sl2 case where it can be checked by direct calculation.
Observing the deep connections between the categorical data of CFTs and Drinfeld’s
quasi-Hopf algebras [47], Frenkel and Zhu asked for a rigorous algebraic bridge in the
framework of vertex operator algebras:
Problem 4. ([61]) A complete conceptual explanation of this phenomenon is needed. In
the setting of vertex operator algebras, associate a general construction of quasi-triangular
quasi-Hopf algebra with a vertex operator algebra with the same tensor category of representations.
Further related conjectures may be found in [65], [128].
Addressing the tensor category structure directly, Huang and Lepowsky constructed a
general tensor product theory for module categories of VOAs [85, 86, 87, 88], establishing,
in particular, the modular tensor structure for affine VOAs at positive integer levels [90,
92, 93]. To reconcile this intrinsic VOA approach with the historical algebraic-geometric
proof of the Kazhdan–Lusztig–Finkelberg equivalence, Huang formulated the following
challenge:
Problem 5. (Problem 4.4 in [97]) Find a direct construction of this equivalence without
using the equivalence given by Kazhdan-Lusztig so that this equivalence covers all the cases,
including the important g = E8 and k = 2 case.
Finally, our operator-algebraic methods based on weak quasi-bialgebras require establishing unique C
∗
-structures on tensor categories. This directly addresses a fundamental
question raised by Galindo [66]:

UNITARY COBOUNDARY WEAK QUASI-HOPF ALGEBRAS 11
Problem 6. May a fusion category admit more than a unitary structure making it into a
unitary tensor category?
In the following Section 2, we state our main results, demonstrating how the construction
of weak quasi-Hopf C
∗
-algebras and the transport of unitary coboundary structures via
Drinfeld twists provides a direct, unified solution to these five problems.
2. Main results
In this section we state some of our main results that we prove in this paper regarding
Problems 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6.
2.1. Unitarizability of fusion categories: The Kirillov and Galindo conjectures.
Let g be a complex simple Lie algebra and let Uq(g) be the Drinfeld-Jimbo quantum group
specialized at the root of unity q = e
iπ/ℓ, with ℓ a positive integer divisible by d, the ratio
of the square lengths of the long and the short roots, and ℓ/d larger than the dual Coxeter
number h
∨ of g. Let C(g, q, ℓ) denote the associated fusion category. Let us define the level
k associated to ℓ by
ℓ = d(h
∨ + k).
Using general Tannakian methods for weak quasi-Hopf algebras, we develop a main
unitarizability criterion for braided tensor categories, Theorem 15.8. A main consequence
is the solution of Kirillov’s Problem 3 given in Theorem 21.2. The proof is based on the
general theorem to the original Finkelberg’s theorem [55], [56] and Wenzl’s unitarity result
of C(g, q, ℓ) [185].
Theorem 2.1. Under the same assumptions as in Finkelberg’s theorem 21.1, O˜
ℓ becomes
a unitary modular fusion category with Kirillov’s Hermitian structure.
Another consequence of Theorem 15.8 is the first positive answer to Galindo’s question
Problem 6 for a wide class of tensor categories with possibly infinitely many simple objects,
the proof is in Sect. 17, see Theorem 17.1. Another proof has been given by Reutter in
[156] with different methods.
Theorem 2.2. Let C1 and C2 be tensor equivalent C
∗
-tensor categories endowed with an
integral weak dimension function (e.g. they are finite semisimple tensor categories). Then
C1 and C2 are also unitarily tensor equivalent.
These theorems are interesting especially taking into consideration the fact that the
abstract theorem 15.8 on which they rely uses only the choice of an integral weak dimension
function on the affine Lie algebra fusion category. This category naturally admits such a
dimension function given by representations of the simple Lie algebra g and no inner
structure is needed in detail, other than the two cited milestone theorems.
Theorem 2.1 is, in other words, a strong motivation to refine our Tannakian methods
compatibly with the inner structure of the categories. This leads us to seek a direct proof
of the equivalence between quantum group fusion categories and CFT fusion categories.
To get a deeper understanding of our unitarizability criterion for affine Lie algebra fusion

12 S. CIAMPRONE, M.V. GIANNONE, AND C. PINZARI
categories, we need to examine their inner structures and utilize their naturally arising
integral dimension functions.
2.2. Lie algebra and level set up. Our framework relies on the setup in [185], which
applies to all simple Lie algebras, whether simply laced or non-simply laced. The fundamental representation V of g is defined in [185] for each Lie type. We also denote by V
the corresponding quantized counterpart of Uq(g), q = e
iπ/ℓ, and the associated object of
C(g, q, ℓ).
The minimal level for which the fundamental representation belongs to the Weyl alcove
is k = 1 for all types g ̸= E8, and k = 2 for g = E8. Accordingly, we assume throughout
this paper that k ≥ 1 if g ̸= E8 and k ≥ 2 if g = E8.
Remark 2.3. The case g = E8 at level k = 1 yields a trivial pointed braided category,
and it is the only case.
Remark 2.4. The sole exception to the constraints in 2.2 is part (c) of Theorem 2.11,
which establishes the equivalence of our structure with the Huang–Lepowsky braided tensor
structure.
Our strategy to prove part (c) of Theorem 2.11 can be viewed as a wide generalization of
the classification of braided tensor structures for pointed categories case (examples of such
categories are described in Subsect. 25.1 for the type A level k = 1 case). To this aim,
we develop a classification of braided tensor structures in a semisimple pre-tensor category
endowed with a generating object, see Theorems 8.13 and 8.21.
By the main result of [185], C(g, q, ℓ) is a unitary ribbon braided rigid tensor category
with natural unitary representation of the braid group.
2.3. Construction of the weak Hopf algebras AW (g, q, ℓ) (global quantum gauge
groups). The weak Hopf algebras AW (g, q, ℓ) summarized in this subsection will be the
solution of Doplicher-Roberts Problem 1 for the braided tensor categories associated to
the WZW model by Corollary 2.9 and Theorem 2.11.
Starting from [185], in Sect. 31 we recall the construction of a strict braided tensor
category G˜
q with objects the fusion tensor powers of V , and we recall an equivalence from
G˜
q to C(g, q, ℓ) with an embedding full functor. Wenzl identified a natural linear functor
W : G˜
q → Hilb.
In Sect. 9 we introduce the new definition of weak Hopf algebra and Theorem 9.10 gives
a categorical characterization by weak tensor functors in the semisimple case.
Moreover, motivated by the hermitian structure of Uq(g) at q = e
iπ/ℓ, the specialization
of Drinfeld coboundary matrix R of the generic Uh(g) [47] and its explicit relation with
the hermitian structure of Uq(g) detailed by Wenzl in [185], we introduce the definition of
compatible unitary coboundary weak quasi-Hopf algebra in Def. 30.1, which replicates the
properties as for the hermitian structure of the non-semisimple Uq(g) in a unitary setting.
We give a new construction extended to all Lie types and levels of such algebras AW (g, q, ℓ)
associated to Wenzl’s functor W.

2.4. Classification of type A braided fusion categories. The following result is our
first application of the weak Hopf algebras AW (slN , q, ℓ) (first constructed in [25]) to the
study of Problems 4–5. It gives an equivalence between fusion categories in type A with
different methods, by classifying ribbon fusion categories with the same fusion rules as
C(slN , q, ℓ) by their ribbon structure. The Grothendieck ring of C(slN , q, ℓ) is denoted by
RN,ℓ.
To establish equivalences between the fusion categories of vertex operator algebras
(which are not inherently unitary) and those of quantum groups or conformal nets, we
replace the unitarity assumption with the weaker condition of pseudounitarity, see Sect.
25.
Theorem 2.6. Let C and C
′
be pseudo-unitary ribbon fusion categories with ribbon structures θ and θ
′
, assumed positive for N even, and with based Grothendieck rings isomorphic
to RN,ℓ with N + 1 ≤ ℓ < ∞. Let f : Gr(C) → Gr(C
′
) be a based ring isomorphism such
that for each irreducible ρ ∈ C, θ
′
ρ
′ = θρ where ρ
′
is an irreducible in C
′
in the class of f[ρ].
Then there is an equivalence of ribbon braided tensor categories F : C → C
′
inducing f. If
the categories are unitary, F may be chosen unitary.

14 S. CIAMPRONE, M.V. GIANNONE, AND C. PINZARI
The previous result is based on Kazhdan-Wenzl theory [110]. The main conceptual
argument of proof consists of Theorem 2.6 in extending an analogous result by Neshveyev
and Yamashita [140] in the setting of compact quantum groups to our weak Hopf algebras.
This result is a first important step in our study of determining braided tensor structures
of categories related to conformal field theory, in that is gives an insight of how the braided
symmetry determines the associator, a feature that will emerge again in our next results
with more direct methods with the uniqueness Theorems 8.13 and 8.21 for braided tensor
structures in a semisimple category with a tensor product bifunctor, a generating object
admitting a positive integer valued weak dimension function.
Remark 2.7. Theorem 25.1 applies to establish in particular equivalence of the categories
C(slN , q, ℓ) and Rep(V(slN )k) giving an alternative proof of KLF theorem in the type A case.
The proof gives information on the structure of the equivalence. The theorem applies also
to other approaches to braided tensor structures in categories motivated by conformal field
theory, provided they have the prescribed fusion rules and ribbon structure.
Kazhdan-Wenzl theory classifies all the ribbon fusion categories with prescribed Grothendieck rings of type A up to a twist of the associativity morphisms. These twists
have been classified by Kazhdan and Wenzl in [110], see also Sect. 25 for more details.
It follows that the categories in the statement correspond to representation categories of
the weak Hopf algebra AW (slN , q, ℓ) up to a twist. Then as anticipated by extending an
argument by Neshveyev and Yamashita to our weak Hopf algebra we prove that the twist
of the associativity morphisms is trivialized by the presence of the braided symmetry. It
is remarkable that their arguments in the setting of compact quantum groups extend to
weak Hopf algebras, because our algebras are not co-associative, strictly speaking. This
contributes to the idea that their lack of co-associativity is limited. We take the opportunity
to note that similar phenomena also occurred in the development of the theory of weak
Hopf algebras Sects. 9, 14, and in the study of amenability in the setting of weak quasiHopf algebras and weak Hopf algebras, see Sect. 16. Moreover, the relevance of weak Hopf
algebras that first emerged in the proof of Theorem 2.6, it emerged again in the direct
proof of Theorem 2.8.
Moreover, Neshveyev and Yamashita proved in the same paper that the categories with
twisted associator in the case where q is real, correspond to compact quantum groups,
and classified these groups, as deformation of the defining compact quantum group. An
analogue of this result for the fusion categories C(slN , q, ℓ) and our weak Hopf algebras
AW (slN , q, ℓ) has been studied in [71].

Theorem 2.11 establishes the equivalence with Huang-Lepowsky structure for simply
laced and non simply laced Lie types. By bypassing the analytic monodromy of the KZ
equations entirely and the Verlinde formula, we establish an alternative construction of
the Huang–Lepowsky braided tensor structure.
Our proof starts with the the work of Wenzl [185] alongside the description of the primary
fields by Wassermann [181] for the minimum energy functor of affine Lie algebras in the
type A case. This leads to the description by Frenkel and Zhu of the fusion rules for the
affine vertex operator algebras [61]. The comparison of the two tensor product befunctprs
results in the tensor product Theorem 34.7. Connection between the work by Frenkel and
Zhu with the basic tensor product bifunctor by Huang and Lepowsky is given in Theorem
33.3. The comparison led us to apply the description of the primary fields and also of
the braided symmetry studied by Toledano Laredo in [169]. For the connection with the
vertex operator algebra setting, see Remark 38.16. Part (a) is proved in Sect. 37. The
corresponding abstract result is explicitly stated in Sect. 15, see also Sect. 7 of [149]. The
rest of part (b) summarizes the comparison on the associators described in Theorems 33.4
and 37.24 and of the braiding morphisms Theorems 33.11, 35.1, Remark 38.16.
In part (c), one needs to extend the coincidence of the braiding and associativity morphisms from the special cases considered in part (b) to all such morphisms. Establishing
this result for a given Lie algebra allows one to pass from local to global coincidence
between our analytic tensor structure and the Huang-Lepowsky structure. Because the
corresponding quantum group fusion category is known to be rigid, this global coincidence
provides a new, direct proof of rigidity for the Huang-Lepowsky braided tensor category.
To prove part (c), we establish our main abstract uniqueness theorem, Theorem 8.21 (a
generalization of Theorem 8.13) for braided tensor structures within a category equipped
with a common tensor product bifunctor and a multiplicative generating object (which, in
our application, is the fundamental representation). This theorem operates via cohomological reduction: by exploiting the pentagon and hexagon equations, we show that the global
braided tensor structure is completely and uniquely determined by its values on a subclass
of special objects, directly treatable in the application. The hypotheses of the theorem are
specifically formulated to match the explicit data available in our setting. Consequently,
our final equivalence between our quantum group based structure and Huang-Lepowsky
structure, holds for all classical Lie types and G2, driven by state-of-the-art results concerning the generalized Schur-Weyl dualities between the fundamental representation and the
braid group within the quantum group fusion category. Ultimately, this approach yields
an independent, algebraic derivation of the Huang-Lepowsky braided tensor structure that
entirely bypasses the historical analytic difficulties of the KZ equations and the Verlinde
formula.

18 S. CIAMPRONE, M.V. GIANNONE, AND C. PINZARI
Building on the results established in part (b), in this paper we verify the hypotheses of
Theorem 8.21 concerning direct comparison between the braiding and associativity morphisms on the side of the quantum group fusion category for its original structure and for
the Huang-Lepowsky structure transferred to the Zhu algebra, via our Drinfeld twist. The
further verification regarding the duality property of the braid group (i.e. the braid group
generates the morphism spaces between tensor powers of the fundamental representation)
is performed on the quantum group side on the algebra AW (g, q, ℓ). This requires separate verification across the various Lie types. To accomplish this, we compare the original
ribbon braided tensor category structure of the quantum group fusion category with the
untwisted braided tensor category structure coming from the Zhu algebra endowed with
Huang-Lepowsky structure. We do this untwist operation applying the same twist as before
on the module category of the Zhu algebra equipped with the Huang-Lepowsky braided
tensor structure from part (a). At this stage, the final assumption of Theorem 8.21 is the
requirement that the centralizer algebras of the truncated tensor powers of the generating representation V in the quantum group fusion category C(g, q, ℓ) are generated by the
representation of the braid group. For the classical Lie types and G2, this verification is
detailed in Section 11 of [149]. It relies on well-known extensions of quantum Schur-Weyl
duality for types A and C, alongside more recent results concerning the generators and
relations of the centralizer algebras for the vector representation in types B, D, and G2.
The detailed proof of Theorems 8.13 and 8.21 and verification of the duality between the
fundamental representation and the braid group is deferred to [149].
Remark 2.12. Theorem 23.10 gives a unitarizability result for RepHL(Vgk). The proof
is based on our approach to KLF Theorem 2.11 for Huang-Lepowsky ribbon braided tensor structure, proved in later sections. This unitarizability result is further specified by
Corollary 2.9 combined with Theorem 2.11 as follows.
Specifically, Corollary 2.9 is obtained as an application of the general unitarizability and
tensor category construction criterion given in Theorem 15.7 for linear C
∗
-categories. To
apply this theorem we choose C
+ to be the linear C∗
-category of unitary representations
of Vgkregarded as a unitary vertex operator algebra (see [73] and references therein for
the notion of unitary representations of unitary vertex operator algebras), A = A(Vgk),
the unitary compatible weak quasi-Hopf algebra structure of the Zhu algebra given by
Theorem 2.8, and C = Rep(A(Vgk)) regarded as endowed with the ribbon braided tensor
structure induced by A(Vgk). The linear C
∗
-structure of Rep(A(Vgk)) is compatible with
that of C
+ via Zhu’s linear equivalence E+ = Z and its inverse S+ obtained by Zhu [197],
since it corresponds to the classical compact real form of g. Theorem 15.7 then applies and
makes C
+ into a unitary tensor category with tensor product bifunctor described in the
proof of 15.7 via the pair (E
+, S+). The unitary tensor category structure obtained in this
way defines RepT(Vgk
) and is also rigid and unitary ribbon braided as so is Rep(A(Vgk
)).
Thanks to the ribbon braided tensor equivalence between RepT(Vgk) and RepHL(Vgk)
by (c) of Theorem 2.11 for the specified Lie types, the unitarizability then holds also for
Huang-Lepowsky ribbon braided tensor category RepHL(Vgk
).

Applicative aspects of hermitian forms of vertex operator algebras and connections with
hermitian forms on the Zhu algebra are discussed in Sect. 23, see in particular the general
criterion given in Theorem 23.9. More examples of unitarization of module categories of
vertex operator algebras fulfilling the needed assumptions are discussed in conclusion of
the same section.
3. Outline of the paper
The first part of the present work is a presentation of the general theory of tensor categories and weak quasi-Hopf algebras, and general Tannakian results both in the algebraic
and C
∗
setting, with or without braiding and ribbon structures. These topics are the
content of Sects. 4–7, 9–14.
Section 9 is dedicated to developing the notion of weak Hopf algebra in a new sense, as
the Hopf algebra counterpart of Mack-Schomerus weak quasi-Hopf algebras. Our definition
differs from the usual notion of weak Hopf algebra, because the coproduct is not strictly
coassociative, but the defining fiber functor is weakly monoidal and the associator explicitly
depends only on the weakness of the coproduct. This includes our previous examples
AW (slN , q, ℓ) of [25] and the new examples AW (g, q, ℓ) constructed later in this paper, in
a general theoretical context.
In Sects. 4, 5 we review tensor categories and their functors. Because CFT fusion
categories are unitary, and are equipped with unitary representations of the braid group,
any reconstruction of quantum gauge group must be equipped with a compatible C
∗
-
structure, which requires considering a new and general notion of an Ω-involution.
Sects 6, 7, 9–13 are devoted to Tannakian duality theorems, both in the algebraic and
unitary case. We introduce a general notion of Ω-involutions for weak Hopf or weak quasiHopf algebras extending and developing the case of quasi-Hopf algebras considered by
Gould and Lekatsas [72].
The notion of Ω-involution describes the unitary structure of our first examples in [25]
in the type A case, with a specific positive matrix Ω explicitly associated to the ribbon
structure and the braiding of AW (slN , q, ℓ), describing positivity of the inner product of
tensor product of representations, and it induces unitary representations of the braid group.
This special kind of Ω-involution is a main interest in later sections of our paper.
Sect. 11 is dedicated to the development of a theory of weak (quasi-)Hopf C
∗
-algebras
with general Ω-involutions, which turns out to unify the theory of the discrete duals of
of the compact quantum groups, where Ω is trivial and the coproduct is unital, with
the unitary structure of the quantum group fusion category C(g, q, ℓ) studied by Wenzl,
associated to the hermitian form of Uq(g) at roots of unity [185].
Ω-involutions arise in Tannakian constructions of quasi-Hopf or weak quasi-Hopf algebras
from tensor C
∗
-categories when the structure maps are not unitary. This property is
inherited by tensor categories equivalent to tensor C
∗
-categories with such maps.
Sects. 14, 15, 26 study unitarizability of braided tensor categories with methods of the
associated weak quasi-Hopf algebras.

20 S. CIAMPRONE, M.V. GIANNONE, AND C. PINZARI
The main result of Sect. 15 is Theorem 15.8, giving a method for unitarizing braided
tensor categories knowing that the category is tensor equivalent to a unitary braided tensor
category.
Section 16 regards amenability, its extends to weak quasi-Hopf algebras known results
from several sources. A main result establishes that when the categorical dimensions are
not integer and the unitary category corresponds to the representation category of a weak
Hopf algebra then necessarily the corresponding Ω-involution can not be trivial. Thus in
cases where one has a trivial Ω-involution then the corresponding algebra can not be a
weak Hopf algebra, see Corollary 16.8.
A main application of the abstract unitarizability results of Sect. 15 is the first solution
to the problem posed by Galindo on uniqueness of the unitary structure, Theorem 2.2 in
Section 17.
We analyse different natural integral weak dimension functions in Sect.18. We apply
Tannakian results to the unitary quantum group fusion categories in Sect. 20. These results
are formulated fixing the choice of an integral weak (i.e. submultiplicative) dimension
functions.
The second main application of the unitarizability criterion 15.8, is Theorem 21.2, giving
the unitarization of the Beilinson-Feigin-Mazur category O˜
ℓ
for all the cases where it is
known that Finkelberg’s equivalence with C(g, q, ℓ) holds [55], [56]. This theorem relies on
Wenzl’unitarity result of C(g, q, ℓ) in [185].
The proof of the unitarizability of O˜
ℓ
, Theorem 21.2, depends on the Finkelberg equivalence, and this was our original motivation to look for a direct proof. We do this in the
rest of the paper.
We discuss our unitarization results of module categories Rep(Vgk) of affine vertex operator algebras at positive integer levels k as a first result on the subject, see Sect. 23.
The preceding Sect. 22 is dedicated to preliminaries on vertex operator algebras and their
modules. This result depends on our main result Theorem 2.11.
Section 24 deals with weak quasi-Hopf algebras obtained via Tannakian duality and
conformal nets, along the same lines.
In the rest of the work we develop direct, canonical constructions of weak quasi-Hopf
algebras naturally associated to the inner fusion structure of the categories via their coproduct.
To approach our study of a direct proof of KLF equivalence theorem, we first study the
type A case, from a classification perspective thanks to Kazhdan-Wenzl theory [110].
We do this in Sect. 25 that can be read independently of the rest of the paper, and the
result is both interesting in its own and quite enlightening for the rest of the paper because
Kazhdan-Wenzl methods are abstract, and thus may be applied to concrete examples
beyond quantum groups, including the case of vertex operator algebras. The main result
is stated as Theorem 2.6 that gives a classification of these fusion categories in terms of
the representation ring and the ribbon structure.
Thanks to the braided symmetry, the emerging categories from the classification can
only correspond to to C(slN , q, ℓ). In particular, the proof shows how any associativity
morphism when realized over C(slN , q, ℓ) becomes trivial thanks to the constraint given

UNITARY COBOUNDARY WEAK QUASI-HOPF ALGEBRAS 21
by the braided symmetry. This result extends a remarkable result by Neshveyev and
Yamashita in the setting of compact quantum groups, Prop. 4.4 in [140]. In our case, we
apply their arguments to the weak Hopf algebras AW (slN , q, ℓ) of [25]. The result highlights
how the presence of the braided symmetry determines the associator and trivializes it for
the quantum group fusion category, setting up the framework for the arbitrary Lie types
handled at the very end of the paper with a mixture of direct comparison, cohomological
reduction, and quantum Schur-Weyl duality.
Our study for the general Lie types beyond type A is starts from Sects. 19 and occupies
the rest of the paper (with the exception of Sect. 25 as said).
To make our approach to unitarization of module categories of affine vertex operator
algebras useful, we first need to understand KLF equivalence in the setting of HuangLepowsky tensor product structure. Our main result is Theorem 2.11 and its proof is a
main aim of this paper.
Sects. 27, 28, 29, 30 develop central categorical and algebraic notions of unitary coboundary weak quasi-Hopf algebras and their compatible and hermitian counterparts. While hermiticity is a weakening of unitarity to the case where the ∗
-involution is not a C
∗
-algebra
involution, and is useful to include Uq(g) as the main example, the mentioned compatibility property is a strengthening to the notion of hermitian/unitary coboundary weak
quasi-Hopf algebra, to the case where, just like Uq(g), the coproduct anticocommutes with
the ∗-involution. The weaker case admits a nice Tannakian characterization established
in Sect. 29 extending to the coboundary symmetry the symmetry property of functors
between symmetric tensor categories, a property envisaged for a long time in our previous
work.
Sect. 32 is devoted to the construction of canonical unitary compatible coboundary
weak Hopf C
∗
-algebras AW (g, q, ℓ) for all the Lie types associated to C(g, q, ℓ), which, as
∗
-algebras, are semisimple quotients of Uq(g), by an ideal which is not a coideal. Their
coproduct naturally arises from the fusion tensor product of C(g, q, ℓ). We study their
naturally arising Ω-involution associated to their coboundary matrices R, extending those
considered in [25], [26] for the type A case with different methods.
Similarly to the type A case, the equivalence between C(g, q, ℓ) and the corresponding
Rep(Vgk) relies on these algebras AW (g, q, ℓ), but in these cases we adopt a direct comparison. Similarly to the type A case, the interplay between the braiding and the associativity
morphisms are relevant. But differently from the type A case, we develop an abstract cohomological reduction for the pentagon and hexagon equations of a braided tensor category
with. a generating object, reminding the classification of braided pointed fusion categories
[50]. We shall be able to explain structural properties of our unitarization of Rep(Vgk) and
its deep symmetry with the quantum group fusion category by the end of the paper.
In Sect. 33 we use these canonical unitary coboundary weak Hopf C
∗
-algebras AW (g, q, ℓ)
to transport, via the construction of a Drinfeld twist of AW (g, q, ℓ), the unitary structure
to Rep(Vgk). To describe this unitary structure, we pass through the Zhu algebra A(Vgk),
which becomes a unitary coboundary weak quasi-Hopf algebra with 3-coboundary associator, by our analytic analogue of the Drinfeld-Kohno theorem 30.4. Up to this point we
have constructed, via transport from the Zhu algebra and Zhu’s linear equivalence and

22 S. CIAMPRONE, M.V. GIANNONE, AND C. PINZARI
its inverse [197], the structure of a rigid unitary modular category on Rep(Vgk
) for all
the Lie types and positive integer levels, with fusion multiplicities transported from the
quantum group fusion category via AW (g, q, ℓ) and the Drinfeld twist. These constitutes
parts (a), (b) of Theorem 2.8. We are left to compare our ribbon braided structure with
Huang-Lepowsly structure.
Thus we pass to introduce Huang-Lepowsky tensor structure in generality, following
historical presentation starting from the setting of affine Lie algebras, where one can see the
first connections between primary fields with the Verlinde fusion structure of corresponding
quantum groups at roots of unity. This is the content of Sects. 34–38 which also contains
the proof of parts (a), (b) Theorem 2.11.
Historically, the comparison between the fusion tensor product of quantum groups and
of affine Lie algebras has roots primary fields, see e.g. [150], or Appendix A in [149]. This
connection emerges in the works by Tsuchiya and Kanie, Kohno, Wassermann and relation
with the work of Wenzl [170], [116], [185], [181].
The notion of primary field and its relation to quanto groups plays a central role in
our proof. Sect. 34 introduces these fundamental notion of primary field in the setting of
loop groups or affine Lie algebras. It gives a central Theorem 34.7 based on the work by
Fenkel and Zhu [61] and our construction of weak quasi-Hopf algebra on the Zhu algebra.
This is the first connection between our truncated tensor product bifunctor ⊠ and what
will later correspond to Huang-Lepowsky bifunctor ⊠HL, via a known connection between
intertwining operators in the setting of vertex operator algebras and primary fields. This
section also contains Corollary 35.1 that is a reduction of the final result to the associator,
using work by Toledano-Laredo [169].
The methods that we have used so far (mainly our version of Drinfeld-Kohno theorem
30.4) to prove parts (a) and (b) of Theorem 2.8 and a direct comparison to prove parts
(a), (b) Theorem 2.11, show coincidence of the two tensor product bifunctors, of the ribbon structure, and the braiding for certain special pairs of variables where one of them
is the fundamental representation V . We also know that special associativity morphisms
coincide when two out of the three variables are the fundamental representation, by direct
verification. We call these special associativity morphisms of CFT type, and we develop an
abstract cohomology theory in Sect. 8 to determine the remaining braiding and associativity morphisms uniquely using the pentagon and hexagon equations. The main results
are Theorems 8.13 and 8.21, whose proofs are deferred to [149].
This general comological reduction applies to the case where the category has a generating object in a given linear category with a tensor product bifunctor, with braiding
morphisms that generate the centralizer algebras of tensor powers of the generating object.
This applies to the quantum group fusion categories for the classical Lie types ABCD and
G2, by results known in the literature on generalization of Schur-Weyl duality, see [24] for
the classical vector representation, and due to Wenzl and his collaborators for the considered fundamental representations. This application is discussed in Sect. 11 in [149] as
well.
In summary, our approach systematically bridges quantum groups with the analytical
realities of conformal field theory. By substituting strict coassociativity with our weak

UNITARY COBOUNDARY WEAK QUASI-HOPF ALGEBRAS 23
quasi-Hopf framework, we bypass the historical reliance on the analytic monodromy of the
Knizhnik–Zamolodchikov equations, passage to negative shifted levels, use of the Verlinde
formula to establish rigidity, use of rigidity to establish braided tensor equivalence. We
also expand our results to all positive integer levels and to all Lie types for our UMFC
structure on RepT(Vgk
) and to the classical Lie types and G2 for the Huang-Lepowsky
structure. This also allows us to uniformly establish the uniqueness of unitary structures
across tensor categories (Theorem 2.2) and settle the long-standing unitarization problem
for the Beilinson–Feigin–Mazur category (Theorem 21.2), together with the other problems
mentioned in Subsect. 1.3 for the WZW model. When classification is unavailable from
the outset, we utilize the weaker condition of pseudounitarity alongside Kazhdan–Wenzl
theory to fully classify Type A ribbon fusion categories and complete the operator-algebraic
proof of the Kazhdan–Lusztig–Finkelberg equivalence.
Perspectives for future research have been discussed in [150]. Among them, the extension
of the theory of quantum homogeneous spaces [146] and induced representations [147] of
Woronowicz compact quantum groups [189, 190, 192] to unitary coboundary weak Hopf
C
∗
-algebras, to study extensions of the WZW model in the setting of vertex operator
algebras and conformal nets.
4. Preliminaries on tensor categories and their functors, generating
object
In this section we recall the basic terminology concerning tensor categories and unitary
tensor categories. Our main references are [50, 137] and [141] respectively. We also give
the main definitions of certain functors between these categories. The most familiar notion
is that of tensor functor but we need suitable weak generalizations, known in the literature
as quasi-tensor functors and more importantly for us their weak versions, the weak quasitensor functors. We also introduce a new notion, that of weak tensor functor between
tensor categories as a slight generalisation of notions already considered in the literature.
We shall describe a cohomological interpretation in the setting of weak quasi-Hopf algebras
later on. Finally, we introduce a notion of unitarity for weak quasi-tensor functors between
unitary tensor categories and discuss a unitarization procedure for general weak quasitensor functors which will be fruitful later on.
All categories in this paper will be essentially small, thus they will admit a small skeleton.
The morphism space from an object ρ to σ is denoted by (ρ, σ). By a linear category C
we mean a category whose morphism spaces are complex vector spaces and such that
composition is bilinear.
The notion of semisimple category is central in this paper, we briefly recall the definition
directing our attention to linear categories, we refer the reader to Ch. 1 in [50] for details.
A linear additive category C is a linear category with a zero object 0, that is (0, 0) = 0,
and direct sums, that is for any pair of objects ρ, σ ∈ C there is an object τ ∈ C and
morphisms S ∈ (ρ, τ ), T ∈ (σ, τ ), S
′ ∈ (τ, ρ), T′ ∈ (τ, σ) such that S′S = 1, T′T = 1,
SS′ + T T′ = 1. The object τ is defined up to isomorphism and denoted ρ ⊕ σ. A linear
abelian category is a linear additive category with extra structure. The central additional

24 S. CIAMPRONE, M.V. GIANNONE, AND C. PINZARI
notion is that of kernel and symmetrically of cokernel of a morphism. For a morphism
A ∈ (ρ, σ) the kernel Ker(A) is an object k and a morphism K ∈ (k, ρ) such that AK = 0,
and universal with this property. Kernels and cokernels are assumed to exist for every
morphism, among other things. A subobject of an object ρ is an object σ together with a
morphism S ∈ (σ, ρ) with Ker(S) = 0. An object ρ is called simple, or irreducible, if ρ ̸= 0
and the only subobjects are 0 and ρ.
It follows from Schur’s Lemma, see e.g. Lemma 1.5.2 in [50] and Prop. 5.4.5 in [27]
that in a linear abelian category with finite dimensional morphism spaces, when ρ and σ
are simple, (ρ, σ) is either the trivial vector space or it is formed by scalar multiples of a
unique isomorphism, it follows that (ρ, ρ) = C1. In our paper, all our categories will have
finite dimensional morphism spaces.
A semisimple category is a linear abelian category such that every nonzero object is a
finite direct sum of simple objects, the decomposition is unique up to isomorphism.
A splitting idempotent, or a summand, of an object ρ is an object σ, an idempotent
E ∈ (ρ, ρ) together with morphisms S ∈ (σ, ρ), S
′ ∈ (ρ, σ) such that S′S = 1, SS′ = E. In
particular, σ is a subobject of ρ. For example, a direct sum ρ⊕σ as previously defined has
ρ and σ as summands defined by complementary idempotents. In a semisimple category
every idempotent splits, thus every subobject is a summand.
The next notion is that of tensor category. We follow Sect. 1.2 in [137], and the notion
of monoidal category of Ch. 2 in [50] except for we assume the linear structure.
Definition 4.1. By a pre-tensor category we mean a linear category C endowed with a
tensor product operation ⊗, which is a bilinear bifunctor C×C → C, a distinguished tensor
unit object ι satisfying the unit axioms, that is the functors ρ → ρ ⊗ ι and ρ → ι ⊗ ρ are
autoequivalences of C.
Definition 4.2. A tensor category is a pre-tensor category endowed with natural isomorphisms αρ,σ,τ : (ρ ⊗ σ) ⊗ τ → ρ ⊗ (σ ⊗ τ ).
The associativity morphisms αρ,σ,τ are required to satisfy the pentagon equation
((ρ ⊗ σ) ⊗ τ ) ⊗ υ

/(ρ ⊗ (σ ⊗ τ )) ⊗ υ

(4.1)
By Sect. 2.9 in [50] one can identify ρ ⊗ ι and ι ⊗ ρ by a simple passage which uses only
the unit isomorphisms, in this way ι becomes strict, meaning that ι ⊗ ρ = ρ ⊗ ι = ρ for
every object and 1ι ⊗ T = T ⊗ 1ι = T for every morphism T. To simplify our discussion,
we shall assume that ι is strict in our abstract results, and we shall tacitly use this passage
in our applications where it is not natural to work with a strict unit, e.g. Sect. 22, 23.
Moreover, we assume the normalization condition αρ,ι,τ = 1.
Alternatively, one may adopt the definition of monoidal units originally given by Saavedra, following the treatment in [115]. This viewpoint has been adopted in [71]. (See also
Remark 25.2 for a brief description of the aims of the thesis and more closely related papers
in the setting of Kazhdan-Wenzl theory, Sect. 25.)

UNITARY COBOUNDARY WEAK QUASI-HOPF ALGEBRAS 25
Here we just point out an interesting and quite handy feature: in terms of Saavedra
notion, a tensor functor F : C → C
′
is compatible with units exactly if it sends units into
units, or equivalently if it sends any one unit, say ι into a unit. Moreover, given any other
unit ι
′ of C′
, the compatibility is expressed by a unique isomorphism between ι
′ and F(ι).
We shall only deal with tensor categories for which the tensor unit satisfies (ι, ι) = C1.
The category is called strict if the tensor unit is strict and associativity morphisms are
identity. With abuse of language, we shall also regard the category Vec of finite dimensional
vector spaces as strict. Whenever convenient, for a given semisimple category C, we fix a
set Irr(C) of simple objects in C such that every simple object of C is isomorphic to exactly
one element of Irr(C). This can be done because we are assuming that C is essentially
small. A semisimple tensor category with finitely many inequivalent irreducible objects
will be called finite semisimple. If C is in addition rigid, it is a (complex) fusion category
[51].
The following definition will play an important role in our paper.
Definition 4.3. Let C be a semisimple pre-tensor category. An object ρ ∈ C is called
generating if every simple object of C is a summand of some tensor power of ρ defined by
some parenthesisation.
A tensor power of ρ is an object defined as the result of iterative applications of the
bifunctor ⊗ to pairs with entries ι or ρ. We define the order of a tensor power of ρ in
a natural way. Different parenthesisations of the same order of two tensor powers of ρ
are equivalent in a tensor category, thus it suffices to verify the generating property on a
preferred choice of parenthesisation in this case, and is independent of the choice of the
associativity morphisms.
Functors between linear categories are C-linear maps between morphism spaces.
Definition 4.4. A linear functor F : C → C
′ between linear categories is called a linear
equivalence if there is a linear functor, called a quasi-inverse, G : C
′ → C such that FG and
GF are naturally isomorphic to the identity functors of C
′ and C respectively.
Remark 4.5. It is well known (Theorem 1 in IV.4 of [123]) that a linear functor F : C → C
′
is a linear equivalence if and only if it is full and faithful (i.e. bijective between the
morphism spaces) and essentially surjective (every object of C
′
is isomorphic to one in
the image of F.) We shall use these definitions interchangeably. When C and C
′ are
semisimple, this is equivalent to the property that {F(ρ), ρ ∈ Irr(C)} is a complete set of
pairwise non-isomorphic simple objects in C
′
.
The following notion of weak quasi-tensor functor for a tensor category was introduced
by H¨aring-Oldenburg in [82] in connection with the study of duality for weak quasi-Hopf
algebras. We note that the definition ignores the associativity structure of C and C
′ and
thus is meanginful in the setting of pre-tensor categories.

Remark 4.10. a) In Sect. 32 we shall construct a weak tensor structure (F, G) on a
certain faithful functor F : C(g, q, ℓ) → Hilb on the strict fusion tensor category C(g, q, ℓ)
associated to quantum groups at roots of unity to Hilb, the category of finite dimensional
Hilbert spaces. To this aim, the notion of negligible morphism in the category of tilting
modules for a suitable quantum group at root of unity Uq(g) plays a central role.
b) In the paper, see e.g. Sect. 8, 33, 37, 38, we shall also consider a notion weaker than

that will be made precise.

c) The following seems an interesting insight gained from the notion of weak tensor
functor, and more specifically from the utility of having the two equations (4.6), (4.7),
which were first noticed in the course of the construction of the examples of weak Hopf
algebras in [25], and then again in the course of the construction of the generalizations of
these examples to all Lie types in Sect. 32. These properties were shown to follow from
the properties of negligible morphisms in the representation category of Uq(g) at root of
unity, but have not been not used in [25] or for the most part of this paper, except for the
following possibly non trivial application.
If we have a faithful functor F : C → Vec on a semisimple pre-tensor category C and a
weak quasi-tensor structure (F, G) for F and an invertible natural transformation
αρ,σ,τ : (ρ ⊗ σ) ⊗ τ → ρ ⊗ (σ ⊗ τ )
making C into a tensor category and we have an understanding in a specific situation of
validity of one preferred equation among the two (4.6), (4.7) on a fixed triple (ρ, σ, τ ), and
if moreover β(ρ, σ, τ ) is a fixed invertible morphism associated to the same fixed triple
(ρ, σ, τ ) and acting a morphism of C between the same objects as αρ,σ,τ ,
βρ,σ,τ : (ρ ⊗ σ) ⊗ τ → ρ ⊗ (σ ⊗ τ )
and satisfies the same property as α among (4.6) or (4.7), then αρ,σ,τ = βρ,σ,τ on that fixed
triple by faithfulness of F and uniqueness of the inverse. If moreover one finds a collection
V of triples (ρ, σ, τ ) for which this property is known to hold on each element of V, which is
sufficiently large to determine α uniquely on all triples of objects of C (for example V is as
in Sect. 8, Corollary 8.15, Theorem 8.13) then β admits a unique extension to associativity
morphisms of C satisfying the axioms of a tensor category, and the extension is given by α.
In our main application, we wish to achieve this setting with F(α) and (F, G) defined by
a Drinfeld twist construction from a situation as in a) and moreover F(β) with the same
(F, G), arise from conformal field theory , see Sect. 38.
Definition 4.11. A tensor equivalence between tensor categories C and C
′
is a tensor
functor E : C → C
′ which is an equivalence of linear categories.
It is known that a quasi-inverse G : C

transformations 1C′ → FG, 1C → GF monoidal see Remark 2.4.10 in [50]. In particular, G
is a tensor equivalence as well.
In general, we are making no assumption on compatibility of α
′ with the two subobjects
of (F(ρ) ⊗ F(σ)) ⊗ F(τ ) and F(ρ) ⊗ (F(σ) ⊗ F(τ )) corresponding respectively to the right
invertible maps Fρ⊗σ,τ ◦ Fρ,σ ⊗ 1F(τ) and Fρ,σ⊗τ ◦ 1F(ρ) ⊗ Fσ,τ .
A weak quasi-tensor functor monoidally isomorphic to a weak tensor functor is itself
weak tensor.

C → C

(4.7) have to be appropriately modified. Equivalently, such functors may be regarded as
covariant (quasi) tensor functors after replacing C
