Theorem 30.5. Let A be a discrete unitary coboundary wqh algebra with compatible ∗-
involution satisfying all the assumptions in the statement and in part c) of theorem 30.4
(e.g. A has strongly trivial involution, see Def. 11.9). Let T be the twist defined in Theorem
30.4. Then T induces a canonical unitary equivalence of ribbon categories Rep+(A) →
Rep+(AT ). Moreover, if A is semisimple then Rep(A) is modular if and only if Rep(AT )
is modular. If A has antipode defined by elements α and β such that β = α
∗
(e.g. of Kac
type) then Rep+(A) is a unitary modular fusion category if and only if so is Rep+(AT ).
31. Wenzl’s unitary structure of C(g, q, ℓ), square root of the quantum
Casimir
Recall that the algebra Uq(g) at complex roots of unity was introduced in Sect. 19,
and we assume the same setting as there. In particular, it becomes a (topological) ribbon
complex Hopf algebra with a ∗–involution.
For the reader convenience we recall two main results from [185] concerning the unitary
structure. They center on a continuity argument that plays an important role in our
paper in connecting structures from quantum groups at roots of unity to corresponding
structures from affine Lie algebras, developed in Sects. 26–33. To this aim, as we shall
see, it is important that the root of unity is minimal of large enough order in the sense of
Def. 19.1, 20.2.
Remark 31.1. Note that for non-minimal roots, the continuity argument may not be
applied, but the construction of this section possibly extend to these cases and lead to
Hermitian structure. This may have possible applications to the study of Hermitian ribbon
categories or applications to Chern-Simons theory, see Remark 8 in [161].
31.1. Unitary structure of irreducible modules. The first result describes unitarizability of a fixed specialized Weyl module Vλ(q) of the Hopf algebra Uq(g) when q varies
in an arc of the unit circle depending on λ. Recall that if q is any complex root of unity, ℓ
′
denotes the order of q, ℓ the order of q
2
, Λ+ the cone of dominant integral weights, Λ+(q)
the open Weyl alcove associated to q, Λ+(q) = {λ ∈ Λ
+ : ⟨λ + ρ, θ0⟩ < ℓ}, where θ0 is the
highest root θ if d divides ℓ and the highest short root θs otherwise. Forthermore, Λ+(q) is
contained in a fundamental domain for the action of the affine Weyl group Wℓ
′. To cover
the case where q ∈ T is not a root of unity, we set ℓ
′ = ∞, Λℓ
′=∞ = Λ+ and Wℓ′ is the
ordinary Weyl group.
Theorem 31.2. ([185] Proposition 2.4) Let λ be a dominant weight and
I = {q = e
iπt : |t| <
1
m − d
}
where m = ⟨λ + ρ, θ⟩. Then Vλ(q) is simple and the Hermitian form defined in Prop. 2.2
in [185] is positive definite for q ∈ I.

188 S. CIAMPRONE, M.V. GIANNONE, AND C. PINZARI
Proof. We claim that λ ∈ Λ+(q) for q ∈ I, that is ⟨λ + ρ, θ0⟩ ≤ ℓ. We may assume that ℓ
′
is finite. Note that |t| <
1
m−d
implies ℓ > m − d as 2πr|t| < 2π for r ≤ m − d. We have
the following cases.
a) d = 1; In this case θ0 = θ, and the claim is m ≤ ℓ, but we already know this.
b) 1 < d and d divides ℓ; In this case θ0 = θ and we need to show m ≤ ℓ. As pointed out
in [185], ⟨µ, θ⟩ is divisible by d for any dominant weight µ, thus m − d < ℓ is equivalent to
m ≤ ℓ,
c) 2 = d and ℓ is not divisible by d; we have θ0 = θs and we need to show ⟨λ+ρ, θs⟩ < ℓ.
We have ⟨λ+ρ, θs⟩ = m− ⟨(λ+ρ, θ−θs⟩ < ℓ+d− ⟨λ+ρ, θ−θs⟩, but ⟨λ+ρ, θs⟩ < ⟨λ+ρ, θ⟩
are both integers thus ⟨λ + ρ, θs⟩ < ℓ + 1 in this case,
d) 3 = d and ℓ not divisible by d; we have θ0 = θs and we verify ⟨λ + ρ, θs⟩ ≤ ℓ. We also
have g = G2, ⟨λ + ρ, θ − θs⟩ = 4 and we conclude as in the previous case, and the claim is
proved. Assume that Vλ(q) is not simple. It follows it admits a submodule of highest weight
µ < λ, thus λ and µ lie in Λ+(q) that is a fundamental domain for the translated action of
Wℓ
′, thus these dominant weights can not be conjugate under this action, contradicting the
linkage principle. The Hermitian form (ξ, η) of Vλ,A′ as in the statement is Hermitian over
the Laurent polynomial ring A′ by Prop. 2.3 in [185] and thus it becomes an Hermitian form
for Vλ(q) specializing x to any complex number q with |q| = 1. Moreover it is not trivial as
(vλ(q), vλ(q) > 0 and it makes Vλ(q) into a ∗-representation, and it follows that the radical
of the Hermitian form is a ∗-subrepresentation, that vanishes by simplicity, and it follows
that the form is non-degenerate. Let vi(q) be the specialization of Kashiwara-Lusztig basis
at q and consider the matrix with entries (vi(q), vj (q)), which depend continuously on q,
and therefore the same holds for the eigenvalues. The ∗-involution of the Hopf algebra
U(g) for q = 1 (classical limit) corresponds to the compact real form gR of g, and the
Hermitian form of the Weyl module Vλ of U(g) is a unitary representation of the compact
Lie subgroup K of G, see Remark 19.2. Hence ((vi(q), vj (q))) is positive for all q ∈ I. □
Remark 31.3. Note that by the proof of Lemma 2.2 in [185], the Hermitian form of the
statement is the specialization at q of an Hermitian form on the Weyl module Vλ,A′ of
U
†
A′(g) satisfying the assumptions in b) of Prop. 14.2.
We summarize some of the results recalled in Sect. 19 and in proof of the previous
theorem in the following corollary that plays an important role in [185] and also in our
paper. Recall that minimal roots of large enough order have been defined in Def. 19.1,
20.2.
Corollary 31.4. Let q0 be a minimal root of unity of large enough order. Then for all
q varying on the arc Tq0,1 of the unit circle clockwise connecting q0 to 1, we have that
Λ+(q0) ⊂ Λ+(q) and the latter equals the whole Weyl chamber Λ
+ for q = 1; for λ ∈ Λ+(q0),
and q varying continuously in Tq0,1, the Kashiwara-Lusztig basis of Vλ,A′ specializes to a
basis of Vλ(q); the invariant Hermitian form of Vλ(q) is positive definite and is uniquely
determined by (vλ(q), vλ(q)) = 1 and is invariant with respect to the compact real form of
U(g) for q = 1.

We next recall the unitary structure on certain tensor products of specialized Weyl

31.2. Square root of the quantum Casimir operator and compatible Hermitian
coboundary structure of Uq(g). Loosely speaking, the strategy of [185] is to define a
Hermitian structure on tensor products Vλ(q0) ⊗ Vµ(q0) for λ, µ ∈ Λ+(q0) via the action of
a coboundary matrix of Uq0(g) on the space. By [161], Uq0(g) is a ribbon Hopf algebra in
a topological sense. However the ribbon element v may not have a square root in Uq0(g)
arising as the specialization at q0 of an element in the integral form U
†
A′(g). This problem
is mentioned at the beginning of Sect. 3.6 in [185].
Indeed, if this were the case then following a central idea in [185], by our theorem 28.9
we would have an Hermitian form on the full tensor product obtained by specialization
of a nondegenerate A′-valued Hermitian form. Since it is obtained as a specialization,
the Hermitian form would be continuous in q, and thus positive since it reduces to the
usual inner product for q = 1. It would then follow that the tensor product is completely
reducible, but this is not always the case.
For our purposes we shall need an Hermitian form defined on every tensor product space
Vλ(q0) ⊗ Vµ(q0) for λ, µ ∈ Λ
+(q0), thus we look for a square root operator of the action
of R21R. Using the ribbon structure given by the specialized quantum Casimir v see Def.
19.5, Theorem 19.7, Sect. 19, 20, 31, we are reduced to construct a square root of the
action of v on a full tensor product.

Remark 31.8. Thus Rλ,µ defines a non-degenerate Hermitian form on the full tensor
product space Vλ(q0) ⊗ Vµ(q0) making the braided symmetry unitary, cf. the problem
mentioned at the beginning of Sect. 3.6 in [185]. On the other hand, not all the idempotents pγ : Vλ,A′ ⊗A′ Vµ,A′ → Vγ,A′ describing classical fusion specialize to corresponding
idempotents pγ(q) by non-semisimplicity. By [185], this is an obstruction in the proof of
positivity of the Hermitian form, in that when this is possible then the Hermitian form of
the specialized module Vλ(q0) ⊗ Vµ(q0) is positive on Vγ(q0) by a continuity argument that
links q0 to the classical limit 1 that comes from specialization (unlike the construction of
Prop. 31.7). Notably, there are sufficiently many modules for which this holds, and in a
nutshell from them we shall construct the wqh.

Remark 31.11. Note that the ribbon element of Uq0(g) defined in detail in [161] acts on
any irreducible Weyl module Vλ(q0) as q0
−⟨λ,λ+2ρ⟩
, but this may not suffice to determine an
expression of the ribbon element in Uq0(g). Moreover, there is no unitary square root of
the ribbon element in Uq0(g) which is continuous in q from 1 to q0 as otherwise by Theorem
28.9 this induces an Hermitian form on any full tensor product of Weyl modules and by
continuity in 1 by Wenzl argument it is positive definite. Thus the tensor product would
be completely reducible, but this is not always the case.
On the other hand, when a Wenzl idempotent pγ : Vλ(q0) ⊗ Vµ(q0) → Vγ(q0) onto an
irreducible Weyl module Vγ(q0) is well-defined, a unitary square root w of the action of
v can be defined as in part b) of Prop. 31.7 on Vγ(q0) and is a specialization at q0 of a
corresponding action in the formal variable x. In particular, this holds for λ, µ, γ in the
open Weyl alcove Λ+(q0).

31.3. Hermitian form on tensor product modules (following Wenzl). We next
recall a second main result in [185] on the construction of an Hermitian form on certain
tensor products of specialized Weyl modules. When we specialize x to a root of unity q,
not all the specialized Weyl modules Vλ(q) are irreducible, and not all the idempotents pγ :
Vλ,A′ ⊗A′ Vµ,A′ → Vγ,A′ describing classical fusion specialize to corresponding idempotents
pγ(q).
For example, when λ, µ ∈ Λ
+(q) then Vλ(q)⊗Vµ(q) is tilting and the decomposition into
indecomposable tilting modules depends in general on the affine Weyl group associated
to q and thus on the order of q
2
. When q0 is a minimal root of large enough order (Def.
20.2, 19.1) there are special cases where λ, µ, γ are fixed dominant weights of Λ+(q0) when
we let q varying continuously from q0 to 1 as in Corollary 31.4, and we regard λ, µ, γ as
elements of Λ+(q) (that contains Λ+(q0) by the same Corollary).
In these cases pγ(q) can be constructed as an idempotent onto the isotypic component
corresponding to γ arising from specialization in q of a corresponding idempotent between
modules of the integral form. Assume that we are in one of these cases. On the range of
pγ(q), ∆(v)
1/2
v
−1/2 ⊗ v−1/2 acts as as a square matrix with respect to the product of the
two Kashiwara-Lusztig bases with entries powers in the specified power q
1/L = e2πit/L (the
weights of the tensor product are congruent to λ + µ, and the action of v on each weight
space is given by the quantum Casimir, part d) of Theorem 19.4, thus ∆(v)
1/2
v
−1/2 ⊗v−1/2
acts diagonally on each weight space with eigenvalues integral powers of q
1/L). Note in
particular that this action is continuous.
Moreover, R(q) acts as a rectangular matrix, as R(q) may not leave the range of pγ(q)
invariant. It follows that Rλ,µ(q) defines a non-degenerate Hermitian form on the product
Hilbert space making the ranges pairwise orthogonal if we in addition know that Vλ(q) ⊗
Vµ(q) is completely reducible into irreducible Weyl modules as in the classical case. Thus
in this case pγ(q) is selfadjoint with respect to the Hermitian form of the domain, and it
follows that the form is nondegenerate on the range of pγ(q), and therefore positive by the
continuity argument. The selfadjointness property reads as
pγ(q)
†Rλ,µ(q) = Rλ,µ(q)pγ(q), (31.2)
where pγ(q)
†
is the adjoint of pγ(q) with respect to the tensor product Hilbert space structure.
More precisely, by Sect. 3.5 in [185], we choose a fundamental representation V of the
Lie algebra for each Lie type. Let κ be its highest weight. This representation can be
defined explicitly as follows (we follow [98] for notation of the fundamental weights here
below):
AN ) the vector module of slN+1, κ = λ1,
BN ) the spin module of o2N+1, κ = λN ,
CN ) the vector module of sp2N , κ = λ1,
DN ) the two spin modules of o2N , κ = λN−1, λN ,
E6) κ one among λ1 or λ6 the corresponding vertex in the Dynkin diagram is chosen
between the two farthest from the branching point,

UNITARY COBOUNDARY WEAK QUASI-HOPF ALGEBRAS 193
E7, E8) κ = λ7, λ8 respectively,
F4) κ = λ4,
G2) κ = λ1.
We shall need the following properties of V . As anticipated in Subsect. 19.1 Wenzl [185]
notes the following useful fact.
Theorem 31.12. Suppose that κ lies in the open principal Weyl alcove Λ
+(q). Then a
U(g)-module is tilting if and only if it is a direct sum of direct summands of tensor powers
of V (q).
Recall that in Subsect. 19.5 we introduced the open Weyl alcove Λ+(q), and recalled
that it labels the irreducible objects of C(g, q, ℓ). We here recall that the Coxeter number
h and its dual h
∨ may are also computed as
h
∨ = ⟨ρ, θ∨
⟩ + 1, h = ⟨ρ, θ∨
s
⟩ + 1, (31.3)
with as before θ the highest root and θs the highest short root. Recall also from Def. 19.1
the level k associated to the order ℓ of q
2
. We introduce
h(λ) := ⟨λ, θ∨⟩ if d|ℓ, h(λ) = ⟨λ, θs⟩ otherwise.
The following result (mentioned in Subsect. 19.1) is simple but useful, especially for the
use of Theorem 31.12, to determine the minimum possible value of the level k for which
the above Theorem applies.
Proposition 31.13. The dominant weight λ lies in the Weyl alcove Λ
+(q) if and only if
h(λ) ≤ k. Moreover h(κ) = 1 for the classical Lie types, E6, E7, and h(κ) = 2 for E8. For
F4 and G2 then h(κ) = 1 if d|ℓ, and h(κ) = 2 otherwise.
The fundamental representation V is irreducible if g is not of type D, and is the sum of
the two half spin representations in the type D case; every irreducible of g is a subrepresentation of a power of V ; the dominant weight of V (or of each summand in type D) lies
in Λ+(q).
We consider the associated Weyl module of Uq(g) denoted in the same way. For λ ∈
Λ
+(q0), Vλ(q) ⊗ V (q) decomposes into a direct sum of indecomposable tilting modules Tγ
Vλ(q) ⊗ V (q) = ⊕γTγ ⊗ C
mγ
with the property that for g ̸= E8, the dominant weights γ appearing in the decomposition
at most lie in Λ+(q), and for g ̸= F4, E8 the decomposition is multiplicity-free in the
classical case. Thus Tγ = Vγ(q) for all γ and the decomposition is completely reducibile
and unique, thus pγ(q) is defined.
For g = F4 multiplicity may arise for γ = λ. In this case, pγ(q) is first defined for γ ̸= λ
then pλ(q) is defined as the complement idempotent.
For g = E8, the summand Tγ may not lie in Λ+(q) for γ = λ + κ with κ the dominant
weight of V . Multiplicity may arise for γ = λ in this case also. In Prop. 3.6 case 2 [185] it
is shown that a selfadjoint projection pγ(q) may be defined, and we shall comment more
on this in the next section, and more precisely in the proof of Lemma 32.18.

These constructions hold for any primitive root q such that the order ℓ of q

is large

For the unitary structure, Wenzl shows that the restriction to the minimal roots Def.
20.2 is essential. The requirement of having large enough order includes the fundamental
representations in the open Weyl alcove for all Lie types and positive integer levels, see

Remark 31.15. For the unitarization result of [185], one only needs λ, γ ∈ Λ

32. Compatible unitary coboundary weak Hopf algebras Uq(g) → AW (g, q, ℓ)
The aim of this section is to prove Theorem 2.5 stated in Sect. 2. The proof is divided
in three parts. In Theorem 32.6, we introduce a full strict tensor category Gq generated
tensorially by the fundamental representation V equivalent to C(g, q, ℓ). We define Wenzl
functor W : G˜
q → Vec on an equivalent concrete realization G˜q of Gq, in Def. 32.7. In
Theorems 32.12, 32.16 we construct a natural weak tensor structure on W and study the
algebraic and unitary aspects, respectively. In particular, the algebraic Theorem 32.12
considers more general roots of unity, and we hope it will be useful in other circumstances,
discussed in [161].
Our first aim is to construct a natural functor W : C(g, q, ℓ) → Vec associated to the
same dimension function D as in Sect. 19, and thus this functor is a particular case of the
former.
To do this, we consider the tensor structure of C(g, q, ℓ) (q not necessarily minimal)
of [185]. This gives rise to the mentioned forgetful functor W, and we shall construct
a natural weak tensor structure on W, and in this way we have a canonical weak Hopf
algebra AW (g, q, ℓ).
When q is a minimal root of unity, the work of [185] implies that Uq(g) is a Hermitian
coboundary Hopf algebra with compatible involution (in a topological sense), see Theorem
31.9. This Hermitian structure underlies the unitary structure of C
+(g, q, ℓ).
To ease notation we shall write R
U
∆(I) in place of π ⊗ π(R
U
)∆(I), and we shall use a
similar notation for other elements of AW (g, q, ℓ) ⊗ AW (g, q, ℓ) that come from elements of
Uq(g)⊗Uq(g).

UNITARY COBOUNDARY WEAK QUASI-HOPF ALGEBRAS 195
Remark 32.1. Note that by the presence of the factor ∆(I), the element ∆(v)
1/2
involved
in (2.2) is diagonal on the irreducible components, and acts as q
−
⟨γ,γ+2ρ⟩
2 on an irreducible

The weak tensor structure is not unique but when we change it then the weak Hopf
algebra changes only by a trivial twist. The special case g = slN will be useful for the
construction of tensor equivalences studied in Sect. 25. We also note that in this case we
recover the example constructed in [25] with a different method.
Recall that the quotient category C(g, q, ℓ) was outlined in Sect. 19. We assume ℓ
′ < ∞.
Remark 32.2. By Lemma 1.1 in [70], composition of inclusion T
0 → T(g, q, ℓ) with projection T(g, q, ℓ) → C(g, q, ℓ) is an equivalence of linear categories. Hence T

32.1. A specific construction, the weak Hopf algebras AW (g, q, ℓ). In this subsection
q is any root of unity of order in the sense of Def. 19.1. We obtain a functor C(g, q, ℓ) → Vec
together with a weak tensor structure (F, G) associated to the same dimension function D
as in the previous subsection, and correspondingly a weak Hopf algebra AW (g, q, ℓ). In the
next subsection we consider the case where q is a minimal root. For this construction we
mostly take into consideration ideas in [185] that we review and extend to a general root
of unity q such that ℓ is in the sense of Def. 19.1. When q is a minimal root, AW (g, q, ℓ)
becomes a unitary coboundary weak Hopf algebra. To do this, as briefly anticipated in
Remark 32.2, we shall introduce a linear category Gq of non-negligible tilting modules
associated to a fundamental representation of g. This category appears implicitly in [185].
In [25] we have shown that Gq has a natural structure of a strict (ribbon) tensor category
when Vec is regarded as strict and q is a minimal root and is unitarily ribbon equivalent
to C(g, q, ℓ). In this subsection we extend this to roots of order in the sense of Def. 19.1
and moreover we shall define a functor W : Gq → Vec and then introduce a weak tensor
structure on W that corresponds by Tannakian reconstruction to AW (g, q, ℓ).

−1
λ,µ are morphisms and are inverse
of each other. Furthermore extending these morphisms by naturality to every pair of
objects we see that they act as F and G respectively. Then we may verify the tensoriality
equation (4.6) for (E, E). To do this, notice that the tensor product ⊗ at right hand side
of (4.6) modifies ⊗ by inserting suitable idempotents pn which may then be disregarded
thanks to (2) again. c) Naturality of F and G as transformations from G˜ → Vec may be
checked with direct computation. Notice also that by construction F and G are natural
as transformations Gq → Vec, therefore by composition G˜
q → Gq → Vec we find that they
are also natural with respect to ⊠. Property d) follows again from (2).
□
Remark 32.13. It follows from part b) of the previous theorem that the composition
Q : G˜
q → T(g, q, ℓ) → C(g, q, ℓ)
of the natural inclusion followed by quotient is an equivalence of tensor categories. In
this way, G˜
q admits a unique structure of a ribbon category in a way that Q is a ribbon
equivalence. On the other hand, this can also be seen directly.

Lemma 32.18. The natural transformation F defining AW satisfies equations (32.9),
(32.10) for k = 1.
Proof. Assume g ̸= E8. For k = 1, by [185], V ⊗Vλ is completely reducible into irreducible
components ⊕µmµVµ (with multiplicity 0 or 1 except for g = F4 where µµ > 1 only for
µ = λ) and we have that µ ∈ Λ+(q). Thus there is a unique morphism idempotent

202 S. CIAMPRONE, M.V. GIANNONE, AND C. PINZARI
onto a maximal non-negligible submodule V ⊗ Vλ → ⊕µ∈Λ+(q)Vµ which then coincides
with FV,Vλ. This uniqueness property and unitarity of the braided symmetries imply that
(32.9), (32.10) hold for k = 1. The case g = E8 is more delicate that the others, and is
not covered by the above proof. In this case we consider the decomposition of Vλ ⊗ V
and of V ⊗ Vλ into indecomposable tilting modules given at page 274 in [185]. Let FVλ,V
and FV,Vλ be the corresponding idempotents onto the maximal non-negligible submodules.
Then it follows from the proof therein and unitarity of the braided symmetry, that (32.9),
(32.10) hold in this case. More precisely, we consider Wl = Vλ ⊗ V considered in the
mentioned proposition of [185] and we pair it with Wr = V ⊗ Vλ, thus Wlis W in [185].
Similarly, for the submodules of Wl and Wr we follow the same notation, with an addition
of a subscript l or r, for example Ml = (pλ + pλ+k)Wl and Mr = (pλ + pλ+k)Wr. The
braiding cU intertwines Wl with Wr, and therefore also Ml with Mr, as each addendum
isomorphic to some Vµ(q) with µ ̸= λ has multiplicity 1. Moreover Ml and Mr contain
a submodule isomorphic to Vλ+κ(q). We note that these submodules, that we denote by
Vλ+κ(q)l and Vλ+κ(q)r are generated by the (λ + k)-weight space, that is one dimensional,
thus cU takes Vλ+κ(q)l to Vλ+κ(q)r. Moreover cU is unitary. It follows from these two facts
that choosing T(q)r = cU T(q)l, cU takes (Mλ)l to (Mλ)r, and therefore cU (pλ)l = (pλ)rcU
and cU (pλ+κ)l = (pλ+κ)rcU . Note that T(q) involve a choice. More in detail, T(q)/Vλ+κ
complements the submodule N(q)
⊥/Vλ+κ(q) of M(q)/Vλ+κ(q) (this is semisimple). By the
property of c, we may choose T(q)r = cT(q)l. □

33. From AW (g, q, ℓ) to a compatible unitary coboundary wqh structure on
A(Vgk)
In this section we are interested in the module categories of affine vertex operator algebras Vgk
at positive integer levels k. This is an important class of vertex operator algebras
associated to affine Lie algebras. Every vertex operator algebra V has an associated associative algebra, called the Zhu algebra A(V ) [197] recalled in Sect. 22. We shall briefly
recall a natural identification of the Zhu algebra in the case of affine VOAs.
The aim of this section is to apply the Drinfeld-Kohno theorem 30.4 and then transport
all the untwisted unitary coboundary structure from AW (g, q0, ℓ) to the Zhu algebra A(Vgk)
via a beautiful continuous path argument that has been discovered by Wenzl in [185]. Using
the methods of Sect. 15, we derive in this way a unitary modular tensor category structure
on Rep(Vgk). In the second part of the section, we describe this structure of A(Vgk) in more
detail.
Summarizing, in this section, we prove part (a) and (b) of the following main Theorem
2.8. Part (c) constitutes a statement about our analogue of Kazhdan-Lusztig-Finkelberg
theorem, Theorem 2.11, in the setting of affine vertex operator algebras with HuangLepowsky ribbon braided tensor category structure, and will be reformulated more precisely in the following section, see Theorem 2.11, where we shall give a first idea of proof
connecting with work by Kirillov. In Sect. 34 we shall study the comparison between the

UNITARY COBOUNDARY WEAK QUASI-HOPF ALGEBRAS 205
braided symmetry of Rep(Vgk
) obtained in this section and the braided symmetry that
arises from CFT on affine Lie algebras, mostly following the exposition by Wassermann
and Toledano-Laredo, in this way we connect ideas of Wenzl on quantum groups with the
setting of loop groups. In Sect. 37 we describe the tensor category structure of Rep(V )
due to Huang and Lepowsky, in Sect. 38 we shall study the comparison between the
associativity morphisms of Rep(Vgk
) obtained in this section with Huang and Lepowsky
associativity morphisms in the setting of vertex operator algebras. To do this, the notion
of primary field of Knizhnik and Zamolodchikov will play a central role.
33.1. Affine Lie algebra gˆ, affine vertex operator algebra Vgk
, Zhu algebra A(Vgk
).
In this subsection we recall some basic facts about affine Lie algebras and their associated
affine vertex operator algebras. We are interested in the case of positive integer levels (see
[103], [61]).
Let g be a complex finite dimensional simple Lie algebra, h a Cartan subalgebra,
α1, . . . , αr a set of simple roots, and A = (aij ) the associated Cartan matrix. Consider the
unique invariant symmetric and bilinear form on h
∗
such that
⟨⟨θ, θ⟩⟩ = 2 for the highest root θ (33.1)
Consider the affine Lie algebra gˆ = g ⊗ C[t, t−1] ⊕ Ck, with k in the center of gˆ and Lie
algebra structure given by
[a ⊗ t
n
, b ⊗ t
m] = [a, b] ⊗ tm+n + k⟨⟨a, b⟩⟩δm+n,0. (33.2)
Let us fix k ∈ C. Every g-module W gives rise to a gˆ-module Wk such that k acts as the
scalar k. For a fixed irreducible g-module L(λ) with dominant weight λ ∈ h
∗
, corresponding
gˆ-module Lk,λ is characterized up to isomorphism by the following three properties,
a) Lk,λ is irreducible,
b) k acts as k,
c) Lk,λ contains an isomorphic copy of L(λ) given by {a ∈ Lk,λ, gˆ+a = 0}.
where gˆ+ = g⊗C[t]t. By [61], Vgk:= Lk,0 has the structure of a vertex operator algebra for
k ̸= h
∨
, the dual Coxeter number and when k is a positive integer, Vgkis a rational VOA,
see also Sect. 22, 23 for more details and references to the original papers. By Theorem
3.1.2 in [61], in this case the Zhu algebra A(Vgk) is canonically isomorphic to a quotient of
U(g) by the two-sided ideal generated by e
k+1
θ
, where eθ is an element in the root space gθ
of the maximal root θ. By Theorem 3.1.3 in [61], the family of modules Lλ,k, where λ is a
dominant weight in
Λ
+
k
:= {λ ∈ Λ
+ : ⟨⟨λ, θ⟩⟩ ≤ k}
is a complete list of irreducible Vgk-modules.
Taking into account the different normalizations (19.1) and (33.1) of the two inner
products of h
∗
, we have
⟨ξ, η⟩ = d⟨⟨ξ, η⟩⟩.

206 S. CIAMPRONE, M.V. GIANNONE, AND C. PINZARI
Recalling the expression of the dual Coxeter number h
∨ given in (31.3), one sees that the
simple objects of C(g, q, ℓ) summarized in Theorem 19.6 and those of Rep(Vgk) are labelled
by the same set
Λ
+
k = Λ+(q)
provided the order ℓ of q
2 and the level k are related by ℓ = d(h∨ + k) and d|ℓ, see Defs.
19.1, 20.2.
33.2. Compatible unitary coboundary structure on A(Vgk
), proof of Theorem
2.8, parts (a), (b). In the following proof of Theorem 33.2 and in the rest of the section,
the root of unity q in the statement is renamed q0, and q denotes complex numbers in a
neighborhood of q0.
Proof. (a), (b) We show that the unitary coboundary weak Hopf algebra AW (g, q0, ℓ) satisfies the assumptions of the introductory part of the statement of Theorem 30.4, and those
in parts a), b) of the same theorem, and that the twisted algebra is canonically isomorphic
to the Zhu algebra. Let A be a selfadjont operator on a finite dimensional Hilbert space,
and let A = A+ − A− denote the spectral decomposition of A with A+ and A− positive
and A+A− = 0. We set
A
1/2 = (A+)1/2 + i(A−)1/2

Remark 33.1. In Theorems 33.6, 33.7 we describe the weak quasi-tensor structure (Z, F0, G0)
of parts (a) and (b) theorem 2.8 in detail. Note that (F0, G0) is note unique as the same
holds for the corresponding structure (W, F, G) of Wenzl functor W : C(g, q0, ℓ) → Hilb.
We have already noted that the possible variations on Wenzl functor are described by
trivial twists of the associated weak Hopf algebra.
Remark 33.2. We give another argument concerning the assumption in part (c) of Theorem 30.4. Let λ, µ ∈ Λ
+(q0), and Fλ,µ(q0) : Vλ(q0) ⊗ Vµ(q0) → Vλ⊗Vµ(q0) be projections
onto canonical maximal non-negligible addenda defined in 32.9.

idempotents pλ(q), with q varying in Tq0,1, see Subsect. 31.3. We have that the quantum
Casimir acts by matrices with entries polynomials in A′specialized at x
1/L → q1/L (the
minimal L-th square root) on the range of Fλ,µ(q) by complete reducibility, and since
this holds for Rλ,µ(q) also, it follows that Rλ,µ(q)Fλ,µ(q) is continuous in q
1/L. Thus for
any continuous function f : R → R with f(0) = 0, f(Rλ,µ)(q)Fλ,µ(q) is continuous. In
particular, following [185], see also Subsect. 19.3, if f(t) = −1 for t ∈ (−∞, −a], for a > 0
sufficiently small and f(t) = 0 for t ≥ 0, then f(Rλ,µ)(q) is the spectral projection Q(q)
of Rλ,µ corresponding to the negative eigenvalues for q varying in a neighborhood U of
q0. Thus Q(q)Fλ,µ(q) is continuous in U. If Q(q0)Fλ,µ(q0) ̸= 0 then Q(q)Fλ,µ(q) ̸= 0 in a
neighborhood of q0. On the other hand in this neighborood we may find values of q for
which Rλ,µ(q) is positive by Lemma 3.6.2 a) in [185]. It follows that Q(q0)Fλ,µ(q0) = 0.

33.3. The coproduct of A(Vgk
). Universality property of ⊠-bifunctor in Rep(A(Vgk
)),
derivation of Frenkel-Zhu isomorphic images of the spaces of VOA intertwining
operators. We next describe more explicitly the weak bialgebra structure of the Zhu algebra A(Vgk
) of Theorem 2.8 (a), and we shall use this description to identify the structure
with that arising from CFT, that is the braiding following the treatment of [113], [181],
[169] and the associator following Huang and Lepowsky tensor product structure, in Sect.
34, 21 respectively.
To compare with Huang-Lepowsky theory, we aim to show in more detail that the
coproduct of A(Vgk
) induces a tensor product bifunctor ⊠ in Rep(A(Vgk
)) with the following
property. Any A(Vgk))-module is also a U(g)-module using the realization of A(Vgk)) as a
quotient of U(g) [61].

Remark 33.4. Frenkel-Zhu condition was first proved by Tsuchiya and Kanie for the
initial terms of primary fields (vertex operators in their terminology) in the case of the
affine Lie algebra associated to sl2 at level k [170]. Similarly, the relevance of Frenkel-Zhu
condition in the statement of Theorem 33.3 is that the space of U(g)-intertwining maps
fF Z that satisfies it is naturally isomorphic to the space of primary fields for the affine

Lie algebra associated to g at level k, that we shall introduce later, see Prop. 34.4 and
the comment following it. This is also a characterization under a natural isomorphism
of the space of intertwining operators MW3

algebras for Wi the Vgk

fields, that are the maps fF Z. Theorem 33.3 and these isomorphisms will be continued in
Theorem 34.7 in the setting of loop groups and in Subsect. 38.2 in the setting of affine
vertex operator algebras at positive integer level. This series of theorems are all related
and central in our discussion.

Remark 33.8. An associator on an arbitrary triple is uniquely determined by its values on
the family V of triples (Vλ, V, V ), (V, Vλ, V ), (V, V, Vλ) under some extra assumptions. This
claim is stated and proved in Theorem 8.21. The claim implies that any other associator on
A(Vgk
) that restricts to a CFT-type pre-associator associated to the same pair (F0, G0), it
must coincide with the associator of A(Vgk
) constructed in Theorem 2.8 (a) on every triple
of representations. We shall find in Theorem 37.24 that also Huang-Lepowsky associativity
morphisms restrict to the same CFT-type pre-associator ΦF0.G0 on V.
Remark 33.9. If one knew that this CFT-type pre-associator is an associator, then it
must coincide with the former associator of A(Vgk
) constructed in Theorem 2.8. It would
also follow that A(Vgk
) becomes a weak Hopf algebra in this way.

34. Loop groups, primary fields, tensor product theorem for initial
terms 34.7
In Sect. 33, Subsect. 33.1 we recalled the basis of affine Lie algebras, the connection
with affine vertex operator algebras and the classification and correspondence between
their irreducible representations. In this section we recall the basic elements of affine Lie
algebra CFT.
A first main result of this section is Theorem 34.7, where we relate the spaces of initial
terms of primary fields with the tensor product bifunctor constructed in Theorem 2.8 a).
The constructions of Verma modules for affine Lie algebras, see V.G. Kac [103] (10.4.6),
and proofs of Prop. 2.1 and Theorem 2.3 in Tsuchiya and Kanie [170] should be regarded
as helpful and preliminary material.
Then with Remark 38.16. we identify the braiding of Rep(Vgk) constructed in Cor.
2.9 and described explictly in Theorem 33.11 with that arising from the setting of CFT,
following the work of Knizhnik-Zamolodchikov [114] and Tsuchiya-Kanie [170] and focusing
on the subsequent developments by Wassermann [181] and Toledano-Laredo, [169], in the
analytic functional setting. It will suffice to apply this identification to special pairs of

UNITARY COBOUNDARY WEAK QUASI-HOPF ALGEBRAS 215
the form (V, Vλ) and (Vλ, V ). These works are in the setting of loop groups, and we start
recalling the basic terminology and some of their results. (See also the works by Gui, [73],
[74], [75], [76], [77] which expand these results and include the setting of vertex operator
algebras.)
The comparison between the braided symmetry of Rep(A(Vgk
)) and the braiding properties in the setting of loop groups will be achieved using the following Corollary 35.1,
discussed in [169], [181],38.16.
The tensor product bifunctor of Huang-Lepowsky [87] (W1, W2) → W1 ⊠HL W2 is explicitly determined by the irreducible modules and intertwining operators between irreducible
modules, and in the case of interest Rep(Vgk
), this is in turn closely related to the study
of initial terms of primary fields. We shall describe these connections in Sect. 37, 38.
34.1. Classification of simple projective unitary representations of LG, connection with the VOA setting. A simple Vgk
-modules Lλ,k corresponds to a projective
unitary representation π of the loop group LG = C
∞(G, T) on a Hilbert space H of positive energy. This is a strongly continuous representation π : LG → PU(H) that extends to
the semidirect product LG⋊Rot(T), where Rot(T) is the rotation group of the circle which
acts on LG by translation. It is required that the infinitesimal generator d of rotations acts
with spectrum bounded below and has finite dimensional subspaces. A simple projective
representation π is uniquely determined by an integer k, the level, and the top level H[0],
a simple representation of G, called the lowest energy subspace in [169]. For the sake of
completeness we summarize the classification result. We have a decomposition of H as a
Hilbert space direct sum

34.3. Primary field, initial term. The important notion of primary field was introduced by Knizhnik and Zamolodchikov [114], continued by Tsuchiya and Kanie [170]. Our
exposition in the setting of loop groups is influenced by Toledano-Laredo thesis [169].
Let V be a finite dimensional unitary representation of G. Then V [t, t−1] is a representation of g ⊗ C[t, t−1
] that extends to g ⊗ C[t, t−1] ⋊ Rot(T) by Rθf(t) = e
−iθt,

Definition 34.1. Let Hi, Hj be simple (positive energy) representations of LG of level
k ∈ N and let Vh be a unitary simple G-module. A primary field is a linear map
ϕ : Vh[t, t−1] ⊗ Hfin
i → Hfinj
that intertwines the action of g⊗C[t, t−1]⋊Rot(T). The G-module Vh is called the charge
of ϕ, the representations Hi and Hj are called the source and target respectively.
We set, for v ∈ Vh,
ϕ(v, n) = ϕ(v ⊗ t
n
) : Hfin
i → Hfinj
.
We represent ϕ as a formal operator-valued distribution

,
where
∆ϕ := ∆i + ∆h − ∆j,
is called the conformal weight (or conformal dimension, scaling dimension) of the field ϕ,
and ∆i, ∆h, ∆j are defined as in (34.1). The intertwining relation of ϕ can be written as
[X(m), ϕ(v, t)] = ϕ(Xv, t)t
m, [d, ϕ(v, t)] = (t

The following form for (34.2) is also useful,
[X(m), ϕ(v, n)] = ϕ(Xv, m + n), [d, ϕ(v, n)] = −nϕ(v, n). (34.3)
Definition 34.2. The equation on the left hand side of (34.2) or (34.3) is called gauge
condition, see [170].
The equation of the right hand side of (34.2) or (34.3) also has an interesting interpretation, see Remark 34.6. It implies the following important grading relations,
ϕ(v, n) : Hi[k] → Hj[k − n], k ∈ Z≥0, n ∈ Z. (34.4)
It follows in particular that ϕ(v, 0) restricts to a G-intertwiner
Tϕ : Vh ⊗ Hi[0] → Hj[0].

Definition 34.3. The G-intertwiner Tϕ is called the initial term of ϕ. We denote by
IG(Vh⊗Hi[0], Hj[0]) the subspace of HomG(Vh⊗Hi[0], Hj[0]) of initial terms Tϕ of primary
fields with charge the irreducible G-representation Vh, source Hi and target Hj.
Proposition 34.4. (Prop. 2.1 in [170]) The initial term Tϕ of a primary field
ϕ : Vh[t, t−1] ⊗ Hfin
i → Hfinj
of fixed charge Vh acting between fixed irreducible representations uniquely determines ϕ.
Thus the linear map
ϕ → Tϕ ∈ HomG(Vh ⊗ Hi[0], Hj[0])
is faithful.

□
Remark 34.8. Primary fields play a central role in [114], [170], and are also called vertex
operators.
On the other hand, the notion of vertex operator is also used in the setting of vertex
operator algebras with a different but related meaning, see Sect. 22, 23. To avoid confusion
we shall use the term vertex operator only in the vertex operator algebra setting. (Please
note also the historical Remark 5.2 in [102] on vertex operators and reference to the original
articles.)
In Sect. 38, we shall describe the corresponding notion of primary field as in Def. 34.1,
in the setting of vertex operator algebras, see Def. 38.4.
In the case of affine vertex operator algebras, primary fields and their initial terms play a
central role for our purposes, the study of the connection between Huang-Leposwky tensor
product theory and tensor product of the representation category of the Zhu algebra as
described in Theorem 2.8. These developments are considered in Sect. 38.
More precisely, in Subsect. 38.1, we shall introduce a subclass of the class of primary
fields now in the setting of vertex operator algebras as defined in [102], [59]. This is the
subclass defined by a vector in the top space of the charge module and as said it corresponds
to Def. 34.1 in the setting of loop groups. We shall also introduce their initial term.
As said, in Subsect. 38.2 we briefly discuss how this notion reduces to Def. 34.1 and we
re-interpret Theorem 34.7 in the setting of vertex operator algebras.
In Subsect. 38.3 we complete the identification taking into account the associativity
morphisms.
Remark 34.9. Note that at this point of the paper, the notation V seems confusing, in
that it both denotes the fundamental representation of a complex Lie algebra g as in [185]
and a general vertex operator algebra as in Sect. 22. When the two notions are used
simultaneously, we shall refrain from using V for a vertex operator algebra. We hope that
consistent use of the notation Vgkfor the affine vertex operator algebra at level k associated
to g, clarifies the possible confusion.
35. Knizhnik-Zamolodchikov differential equations, and the braiding
The conformal Hamiltonian determines the braiding of Rep(Vgk) explicitly. In the setting
of loop groups, formulae for the braiding may be found in [181] in the type A case and

UNITARY COBOUNDARY WEAK QUASI-HOPF ALGEBRAS 221
see also Chapter 9 Lemma 6.1 in [169]. The fusion tensor product is defined via Connes
fusion, a tensor product operation of bimodules over von Neumann algebras [28]. The
braiding gives rise to certain commutation properties satisfied by four-point functions
of primary fields, called braiding properties in [181], [169] that reflect the action of the
braiding operators in the category of projective finite energy representations with Connes
fusion tensor product. We are interested to unravel the action of the braiding operators
on the corresponding lowest energy subspaces. In this way we shall be able to compare
the braiding of Rep(Vgk
) arising from the setting of loop groups directly with the braiding
that we obtain from our construction in Theorem 2.8 (a) that in turn has been unravelled
in Prop. 33.11.

called abelian braiding in [181], [169]. We thus have the following corollary of this lemma
that is implicit in [169].

222 S. CIAMPRONE, M.V. GIANNONE, AND C. PINZARI
Corollary 35.1. Let H2 and H3 be finite energy unitary projective representations of
LG such that Connes fusion tensor product is multiplicity-free. Then braiding operator
c(H2, H3) acts between the corresponding lowest energy spaces V2 and V3 respectively as
Σe
iπ
2(k+h∨)
Ω
: V2 ⊗ V3 → V3 ⊗ V2 (35.1)
where Ω acts as multiplication by C2+C3−C4 on a simple module V4 of the tensor product.
Proof. The proof follows from a decomposition of V2 ⊗ V3 into simple representations V4
and the previous discussion. □
Note that formula (35.1) is in [113], without details on the eigenvalues of Ω. On one
hand, regardless the multiplicity-free property, a braiding is completely determined by
the operators c(V, Vλ), with V the generating representation, by Prop. 5.5, provided the
associator is determined.
On the other hand, we note that in Prop. 33.11 we have derived the same formula for
the braiding as (35.1) for the special braidings c(V, Vλ), and we recall that the proof of this
fact relies on the analysis of Lemma 3.6.2 in [185].
36. Interlude and organization of the next steps
In Sect. 8 we outlined our strategy for the proof Theorem 2.11. Recall Def. 8.8 of
V-preassociator of CFT-type. In Sect. 19 we started to develop and apply the strategy to
quantum group fusion categories. Our aim is to complete the proof of Theorem 2.8.
So far regarding this theorem, we have worked on the side of quantum groups. We have
constructed a unitary coboundary weak quasi-bialgebra structure on A(Vgk), which induces
a unitary ribbon modular tensor category structure on Rep(A(Vgk)). We have proved parts
(a) and (b) of Theorem 2.8. In Theorem 33.7 we have shown that the associator of A(Vgk)
induced from the quantum group, restricts to a V-preassociator of CFT-type, building on
the work of Wenzl on the unitary structure of the fusion category. We need to prove part
(c) of the same theorem.
On the side of vertex operator algebras, Huang and Lepowsky have introduced and
constructed a vertex modular tensor category structure in module categories of a general
class of vertex operator algebras [85], [86], [87], [88]. They have shown that a vertex
modular tensor category structure induces in a natural way a modular tensor category
structure on the same category [84].
In Sect. 37 we sketch a description of Huang-Lepowsky vertex tensor category structure
and the associated tensor category structure. Let us consider their ribbon tensor category
structure in the case of Rep(Vgk), with k a positive integer. This special case has been
studied in [89].
Part (c) of Theorem 2.8 is reformulated in Theorem 2.11 (c), and diagram (2.4) describes
the methods that we intend to use to study this part. The proof of tensor preserving
equivalence of the first map of the diagram (2.4) will be considered in Subsects. 37.3, 37.4.
We refer the reader to [149] for further explanations on this program, and for the developments of the abstract aspects of the original remark.

UNITARY COBOUNDARY WEAK QUASI-HOPF ALGEBRAS 223
We were not aware at the time of working on our paper, of the beautiful paper [127]2
where transport methods similar to these of our Sect. 15 are developed. Note that McRae
does not use of weak quasi-Hopf algebras, but we strongly advise this paper. For the
present paper we follow methods as discussed in the last years for this project, and we
have kept the original arguments in the presentation of Sects. 37, 38.
The arguments are as follows. The mentioned proof will break in two steps. The first
step transports Huang-Lepowsky vertex ribbon tensor category structure of Rep(V ) to the
representation category of the Zhu algebra Rep(A(V )) via Zhu’s functor Z, and this is the
content of Subsect. 37.3, except for the braiding that is considered separately in Sect. 34
and relies on Toledano-Laredo exposition in the application V = Vgk
.
The second step compares this transported vertex tensor category structure of the Zhu
algebra for V = Vgk with the structure arising from side of quantum groups summarized
in the first two paragraphs of this section.
The comparison with the transported associativity morphisms of Huang and Lepowsky
relies on the notion of primary field introduced by Knizhnik and Zamolodchikov [114], the
work Tsuchiya and Kanie and Fenkel and Zhu in Subsect. 37.3, which reduces infinite
dimensional representations to finite dimensional representations of the Zhu algebra in a
natural (vertex) tensor categorical way. This is a specific property of the model and the
identification is given by certain linear maps, part of a natural transformation, that uses
the clarity of the work by Wenzl on the unitary structure on triples of representations
of which two terms are the fundamental representation and the remaining term is an
arbitrary irreducible, for all Lie types on the side of quantum groups, which we show to be
a significative class of triples. On the side of conformal field theory, the identifying linear
maps uses the lack of logarithmic terms of certain solutions of a one-variable KZ equation,
considered in [170] and [91]. This indicates that an approach via unitarity is parallel to
that vis the KZ equation.
The comparison between the Huang-Lepowsky associator on the Zhu algebra and the
CFT-type pre-associator on the same algebra from Theorem 2.8 is discussed in Subsect.
37.4. This part relies on the fact that the two tensor product bifunctors may be identified.
At the level of affine Lie algebras, an explanation of this identification was given in Theorem
34.7, and already at this level in turn relies on the central notion of primary field first
introduced by Knizhnik and Zamolodchikov and studied by Tsuchiya and Kanie [114],
[170]. In the setting of vertex operator algebras, identification of the two tensor product
bifunctors on the Zhu algebra again based on the corresponding notion of primary field
will be discussed in Sect. 38.
More precisely, to discuss tensor equivalence of the second step we first need to compare
the two pre-tensor structures. Theorem 34.7 relates the spaces of initial terms of primary
fields in the setting of loop groups with the tensor product of Rep(A(Vgk)) constructed in
Sect. 33. To make this result useful, we need to relate primary fields in the setting of loop
groups with primary fields in the setting of vertex operator algebras. This will be done in
2CP is grateful to M. Yamashita for a discussion leading to [127]

224 S. CIAMPRONE, M.V. GIANNONE, AND C. PINZARI
Sect. 38. Then the equivalence of the two associativity morphisms follows from the fact
that Huang-Lepowsky associativity morphisms on the Zhu algebra is of CFT-type.
In Subsect. 38.1 we discuss the notion of primary fields in the setting of vertex operator
algebras. In Subsect. 38.2 we discuss the correspondence with the setting of Sect. 34 and
re-interpret Theorem 34.7 in this setting. This gives a correspondence of the two pre-tensor
category structures on the Zhu algebra.
Then we will identify the two tensor product bifunctors on Rep(A(Vgk
)) arising from
Huang-Lepowsky tensor product theory and from Theorem 2.8.
Since the second and last maps of the diagram (2.4) are a ribbon tensor equivalences
by the indicated Theorems, completion of the proof of Theorems 2.8 (c) and also of the
main Theorem 2.11 reduces to prove the identification of the associativity morphisms and
braiding morphisms of the first map in (2.4) by comparing the braided tensor category
structure on Rep(A(Vgk
)) obtained in parts (a) and (b) of the same Theorem 2.8 with
those of the braided tensor category structure on Rep(A(Vgk
)) induced by Huang-Lepowsky
braided tensor category structure of Rep(Vgk).
Regarding the comparison of the two associativity morphisms and the two braiding morphisms on Rep(A(Vgk
)), we recall that on the side of quantum groups, Theorem 33.7 gives
the explicit evaluation of the associator of A(Vgk
) derived from Theorem 2.8 (a) on the special triples of representations with two terms occupied by the fundamental representation
of the classical Lie algebra and the remaining term occupied by an arbitrary irreducible
representation in the Weyl alcove. The theorem describes this associator as an associator
extending the V-pre-associator of CFT-type associated to (F0, G0) and to the collection V
of these special triples. For the braided symmetries, theorems 33.11 and Corollary 35.1
identify the braiding morphisms for all pairs with one term occupied by the fundamental
representation. We show in Subsect. 37.4 that the associator on Rep(A(Vgk)) arising from
Huang-Lepowsky tensor category structure restricts to the CFT-type pre-associator associated to (F0, G0) on the same special triples V. The conclusion will follow as an application
of the uniqueness Theorem 8.21, provided the assumption on the generating properties of
the braid group are satisfied. These assumptions have been verified in Sect. 11 of [149],
based on the existing literature, and leads to the restricted Lie types as stated.
We note that an interesting problem is to develop more explicitly the connection between
our unitary structure on the Zhu algebra, the CPT operator and also with the papers by
Gui, a statement of which may be found in 2.8 (d). The Hermitian form of the unitary wqh
A(Vgk) conjecturally coincides with that of Gui in the setting of vertex operator algebras
[73, 74]. We postpone the development of Theorem 2.8 (d).

tensor category has a naturally associated tensor category as described in Sect. 4 of [84].
In this section we describe this tensor category structure in more detail.

Intertwining operators and P(z) intertwining maps are closely related. Roughly speaking, a P(z)-intertwining map results from an intertwining operator by evaluation on a
nonzero complex number z. Along this procedure, the charge and source spaces do not
change, but the range is replaced by W3. The following is a more precise description.
Let log(z) be the principal branch of the complex logarithm function with the complex
plane cut along the positive real axis, that is
log(z) = log(|z|) + i arg(z), 0 ≤ arg(z) < 2π,
and for p ∈ Z, define the branches
lp(z) = log(z) + 2πpi.

A central property for intertwining operators, called associativity of intertwining operators, a property relating products with iterates. Associativity is very important, and
draws its origin from the Operator Product Expansion, introduced by Belavin, Polyakov
and Zamolodchikov in 2-dimensional conformal field theory [12].
Remark 37.8. Associativity of intertwining operators is referred the (Non-meromorphic)
Operator Product Expansion (OPE) of chiral vertex operators, in the works by Huang and
Lepowsky. Note that in physics literature one often meets OPE for primary fields. Since
primary fields are of main interest for our purposes, to avoid possible confusion, we shall
refer to Huang-Lepowsky associativity as OPE of intertwining operators. See also Def.
37.12 for a reformulation in terms of irreducible modules.

UNITARY COBOUNDARY WEAK QUASI-HOPF ALGEBRAS 229
Remark 37.10. A term of the form of the left hand side of (37.18) is a series with the
summation developed over the graded subspaces of the target of Y2 (or the source of Y1),
and converges absolutely in the weak topology defined by the source of Y2 and the restricted
dual of the target of Y1 if and only if the series at the right hand side of (37.18) converges
absolutely weakly as well. A property implying these equivalent conditions is developed
in [88] and called convergence and extension property. The property is also formulated in
Def. 3.4 in [91]. In that paper, the convergence and extension property has been derived
under certain conditions. In particular in the same paper the author showed that it holds
under the rationality conditions a), b) c) of Sect. 23. Moreover, it was shown in [88] that
associativity of intertwining operators follows from the convergence and extension property.
Thus the convergence and extension property is a main analytical step to construct the
structure of a braided tensor (in fact a braided vertex tensor) category on the category
of modules of a vertex operator algebra. The detailed discussion and derivation of this
property and its connection to systems of differential equations may be found in [91]. We
shall only be concerned with some simple aspects of convergence properties that will be
useful for us to expose the connection between Huang-Lepowsky associativity morphisms
and that arising from quantum groups (cf. Theorem 2.8) in the case of affine vertex
operator algebras.

37.3. Transporting Huang-Lepowsky vertex tensor category structure to the
Zhu algebra. In this subsection we describe the first step mentioned in Sect. 36, useful
to construct the first tensor equivalence in (2.4). Recall that the first step aims to transport
the vertex tensor category structure of Rep(Vgk) to the Zhu algebra, and preserves all the
structure by construction.
To study associativity morphisms of module categories of vertex operator algebras and
the transported structure to the Zhu algebra, we find it very clarifying the following passage
from composition of intertwining operators to composition of Huang-Lepowsky intertwining maps. This passage is analogous to the case of intertwining operators, see Prop. 37.4,
and an important step to the construction of vertex tensor product theory. We next sketch
Huang-Lepowsky associativity morphisms αHL of the vertex tensor category.

38. Primary fields for affine VOAs, products, iterates, initial terms
In this section, we complement the discussion of the associativity morphisms of the
Huang-Lepowsky tensor product theory in the case of an affine vertex operator algebra Vgk
at a positive integer level k. Central notions are composition of primary fields operators.
As for primary fields themselves, compositions are uniquely determined by restriction and
compression to top level spaces. This gives rise to a single variable operator valued function
that solves a linear one variable differential equation, known as the reduced KZ equation
first studied in [114], [170]. The initial term is understood in the setting of a single variable
differential equation with regular singular points.
In the setting of vertex operator algebras, we are interested in a subclass of the class
of primary fields following the definitions in [59]. Primary fields in the sense of [59] are
intertwining operators evaluated on a lowest weight vector w of the charge module for the
Virasoro algebra (i.e. w is an eigenvector of L0 and satisfies Lnw = 0, n > 0). The subclass
we are interested in satisfies in addition the requirement that w has the lowest eigenvalue
of the action of L0 on the module.
In other words, if W is an irreducible charge module for an intertwining operator Y, we
are interested in the primary field Yr obtained restricting Y to W(0). These correspond to
the primary fields in conformal field theory [162], and also correspond to those discussed
in several papers in mathematics cited in the introduction of [89], to which we refer the
reader.
The primary field Yr in turn gives rise to an initial term Y0 that we define as a linear
map from a tensor product of two lowest weight spaces of irreducible modules to a lowest
weight space of another irreducible module, that is our main object of study.
We may summarize the main ideas as follows. The space of intertwining operators
between three irreducible Vgk modules is naturally isomorphic to the space of primary
fields as above of the same type. We transport Huang-Lepowsky associativity morphism
to primary fields, and from this to the operator algebra of products of primary fields
associated to the OPE of primary fields, and finally to the spaces of their initial terms. In
this way, we are working on the Zhu algebra.

240 S. CIAMPRONE, M.V. GIANNONE, AND C. PINZARI

In the next subsection, as anticipated in in Remark 34.8, we discuss the fact that in
the case of the affine vertex operator algebra Vgkassociated to a loop group LG, the
two definitions of primary field Def. 34.1, 38.4 and initial terms Def. 34.3, 38.5 are
in correspondence. Moreover, in Remark 38.8 we give a re-interpretation of the tensor
product Theorem 34.7 in the setting of vertex operator algebras.
38.2. Primary fields of the affine vertex operator algebra Vgk
. On a conjecture
by Moore and Seiberg. In Sect. 33, Subsect. 33.1 we have recalled the basic facts of
an affine Lie algebra gˆ and the associated affine vertex operator algebra Vgk with level k a
positive integer, and their irreducible V -modules.
In Sect. 34 we have recalled the basis of loop group CFT, and we have introduced the
notion of primary field following [181] [169].
In Subsect. 38.1 we have discussed primary fields for a vertex operator algebra associated
to a vector in the charge space of lowest eigenvalue for the conformal Hamiltonian L0. The
following result is well known. Since it will be useful to us, we sketch of proof.
Theorem 38.7. If V = Vgk
, under Frenkel-Zhu correspondence of affine Lie algebras
and affine vertex operator algebras, the two notions of primary field given in Sect. 34 and
Subsect. 38.1 coincide. In this correspondence ϕ becomes Yr and the initial term Tϕ becomes
Y0. Thus Y0 determines Yr and Y. The space of initial terms Y0 has been characterized
in Theorem 2.3 in [170] for sl2 and Theorem 3.2.3 in [61] for a general g, and described
in terms of a universal tensor product property of modules of A(Vgk) in Theorem 34.7.
Moreover, the maps Y → Yr, Yr → Y0 are linear isomorphisms.
Proof. We need to take into account the correspondence between affine Lie algebras and
affine vertex operator algebras established by Frenkel and Zhu [61]. We have already
discussed injectivity of Y → Yr in a general setting in Remark 38.3 and of ϕ → Tϕ for a
primary field in the setting of affine Lie algebras in Prop. 34.4. One needs to verify that
a primary field in the sense of Sect. 34, Subsect. 34.3, is the restriction of an intertwining
operator, for a discussion see Sect. 8 in [74] with reference to the original works. The
space of initial terms Y0 has been characterized by Frenkel and Zhu [61], and this gives
surjectivity of Y → Y0. Validity of (34.3) follows from Jacobi identity for intertwining
operators. We have discussed derivation of the equation of motion in Remark 34.6 and
Subsect. 38.1. In theorem 34.7 we have discussed a description of the space of initial terms
using the coproduct of the Zhu algebra. □

Remark 38.8. We remark here on a version of Theorem 34.7 in the setting of vertex
operator algebras. We conclude with a structure that seems closely related to MooreSeiberg conjecture on quantum groups arising from conformal field theory, in Section 9 in
[130].
The initial term Y0 of an intertwining operator Y defined in Subsect. 38.1, may have
a non-trivial kernel. Motivated by Theorem 34.7 in the case of affine Lie algebras, one
may want to use this fact to extend the theorem to a more general situation of vertex
operator algebras. A necessary condition for the vertex operator algebra is the existence
of a complemented subspace of the kernel of Y0 when W3 varies that is compatible with
the vertex operator algebra action. This would lead to a description of Y0 with W3 fixed as
we have obtained in Theorem 34.7 in the case of affine vertex operator algebras, and the
construction of an associated pre-tensor category structure on Rep(A(V )). A necessary
condition for the existence of this complemented subspace is the assumption that Rep(V )
satisfies the weak dimension function property defined in (7.3) in Def. 7.8 with respect
to Huang-Lepowsky tensor category structure. Let us assume for a moment that the
weak dimension function property holds and that a choice of a complemented subspace
for the kernel of Y0 when W3 varies compatible with the vertex operator algebra is done
for each pair of irreducible modules. Consider the construction of Prop. 38.6 extended to
W1 ⊠P(z) W2 by additivity on the irreducible modules W3. That is, let as in Subsect. 37.4

38.3. Top level series for products and iterates of intertwining operators of
vertex operator algebras and initial terms (solutions of the reduced ODE KZ
equation). Connection with the pre-tensor structure of Rep(A(Vgk)). In this subsection we define an analogue of the construction of the initial terms from an intertwining
operator to a primary field in Subsect. 38.1, for a product or iterate of intertwininig operators, see the definitions in (37.15), (37.16). We consider the case where the formal
variables x1 and x2 are replaced by nonzero complex numbers z1 and z2 respectively. The
main difference the construction of Y0 for a single primary field are certain convergent
Laurent series of a single variable for a composition primary fields or associated iterates.
Of these series we consider the leading terms, and refer to them again as the initial terms
of products (and iterates) following the terminology of [170] in the setting of affine Lie
algebras. They turn out to be initial terms of linear differential equations with regular
singular points. We shall indicate further interesting reading.
In some more detail, we define two important one variable convergent power series.
The first was originally studied by Tsuchiya and Kanie [170] and draws its origin in the

UNITARY COBOUNDARY WEAK QUASI-HOPF ALGEBRAS 245
compositions of primary fields and KZ equation in 2-dimensional conformal field theory
[114]. In their work appear as 4-point correlation functions associated to primary fields
and vectors in the charge spaces. We write them down in an equivalent operatorial form.
These series also play an important role in the work by Huang and Lepowsky to establish
associativity of intertwining operators as in Def. 37.9 (the Operator Product Expansion in
the setting of vertex operator algebras) [88]. These series are important because similarly
to the case of the initial term of a single primary field, they determine the composition
(and iterate) uniquely for affine Lie (vertex operator) algebras, see Remark 38.10 for further
information.
These computations have a twofold purpose. On one hand as said they are of expository
nature on a very small part of the analytic work on convergence properties of the series
arising from the OPE. The main result of this subsection is the application to the case of
affine vertex operator algebras discussed in Corollary 38.13, where we discuss an application
to the weak quasi-bi-algebra structure of the Zhu algebra constructed in Theorem 2.8. The
reader may compare this result with Prop. 9.5 of [113].

Remark 38.10. The top level series of the product of primary fields has been studied
analytically in [170] in the setting of affine Lie algebras for g = sl2 (called vertex operators
in their work). They are an important tool to study products. They have been extended
by Huang and Lepowsky in the setting of vertex operator algebras in greater generality, see
Remark 37.10 for some references. The series converges for |z1| > |z2| > 0 and the limit
defines a weakly holomorphic multivalued operator valued function, which for V = Vgk
determines the product (Y1)r(Y2)r uniquely [170], [61], [89], and hence also (Y1Y2) [59].
Moreover, the one-variable function of ζ defined between square brackets of (38.10) setting
ζ = z2/z1 satisfies a reduced KZ differential equation, see Prop. 4.3 in [170] which is a first
order linear ordinary differential equation with regular singular poins at ζ = 0, ζ = 1. The
initial term of the top level series in the sense of Def. 38.9 is also the initial term as a
solution of the reduced KZ equation at ζ = 0, and determines the solution uniquely. Thus
the initial term of the top level series determines the product the corresponding primary
fields (Y1)r(Y2)r (or intertwining operators) uniquely for V = Vgk
. The more symmetric
notion of intertwining operator in the setting of vertex operator algebras allows to study
iterates on a symmetric basis and they are an important tool in the work by Huang and
Lepowsky to study convergence and extension properties. We would like to advertise the
paper by McRae [127]. This paper, having among other things several points in common
with the approach of our paper, is a good complement and expands parts that we do not
consider in detail.
We next focus on a corresponding symmetric notion of top level series and initial term
for an iterate.

Proposition 38.14. Let (W1)(0), (W2)(0), (W3)(0) be a given triple of irreducible representations of the Zhu algebra A(Vgk
), and let us fix orthonormal bases vα, vβ, vα′, vβ′ of
the morphism spaces of the Zhu algebra as in (38.19), (38.20), (38.21), (38.22), resp. and
correspondingly intertwining maps Fα, Fβ, Fα′, Fβ′ as in (38.23), (38.24).
a) If the initial term of the top level series of Huang-Lepowsky products Fα ◦ 1 ⊗ Fβ
expands into a linear combination of initial terms of the top level series of HuangLepowsky iterates Fβ′◦Fα′⊗1 then this expansion is given by associativity morphisms
α(W1)(0),(W2)(0),(W3)(0) : ((W1)(0) ⊠ (W2)(0) ⊠ (W3)(0) → (W1)(0) ⊠ ((W2)(0 ⊠ (W3))(0)
given by the evaluation on the given triple of the CFT-type pre-associator
α(W1)(0),(W2)(0),(W3)(0) = (F0)1,2(G0)2,1.
b) Similarly, if the initial term of iterates Fβ′◦Fα′⊗1 expands into a linear combination
of initial terms of products Fα ◦ 1 ⊗ Fβ then this expansion is given by associativity
morphisms
β(W1)(0),(W2)(0),(W3)(0) : (W1)(0) ⊠ ((W2)(0) ⊠ (W3)(0)) → ((W1)(0) ⊠ (W2)(0)) ⊠ (W3)(0)
given by
β(W1)(0),(W2)(0),(W3)(0) = (F0)2,1(G0)1,2.
c) Conversely, if the embedding of the initial term of the top level series of a product
Fα◦1⊗Fβ (or an iterate Fβ′ ◦Fα′⊗1 resp.) in Vec is compatible with that of an iterate on a specific triple ((W1)(0),(W2)(0),(W3)(0)) then the CFT-type pre-associator
(F0)1,2(G0)2,1 (or (F0)2,1(G0)1,2 resp.) induces linear expansions as above.
Proof. a) The elements at the right hand side of (38.26) are invariant under right multiplication by (G0)2,1(F0)2,1 by right invertibility of F0. Thus if the desired expansion exists for a
given triple ((W1)(0),(W2)(0),(W3)(0)) then we necessarily have the compatibility condition
described in the statement,

c) Conversely, the identification of the initial term of top level series of products of
Huang-Lepowsky intertwining operators with linear maps in the non-strict tensor category
of vector spaces requires right multiplication on the right of by the associativity morphism
of Vec. Since this is a morphism in the category of representations of a classical Lie algebra
satisfying in addition an k-constrained truncated fusion rules, under the realization of the

tensor product bifuntor ⊠ of the representation category of the Zhu algebra, the initial
term of a product must be multiplied by (G0)2,1(F0)2,1, cf. the equality (38.27). We may
now apply the computations (38.28). □
Remark 38.15. Note that the compatibility condition refers to a specific triple, and in
that case we write down the associativity morphism of the Zhu algebra as pre-associator
of CFT-type. It may not be easy to construct a pre-associator of CFT-type which satisfies
all the axioms of a tensor category (i.e. to solve the problem of whether the Zhu algebra
can be made into a weak Hopf algebra). On the other hand, the quantum groups constructions imply that the Zhu algebra admits a V-pre-associator of CFT-type and that the
corresponding unique associator is equivalent to Huang-Lepowsky theory, since it is so on
the collection V.
We conclude with sufficient conditions under which the assumptions of a) in Prop. 38.14
hold. These are the conditions under which elements at the right hand side of (38.25)
admit a linear expansion in terms of elements at the right hand side of (38.26). This
will give an application of Theorem 37.24 on the determination of the OPE constants

Recall that V here denotes the fundamental of the classical Lie algebra g chosen as in

Recall also that ((W1)(0),(W2)(0),(W3)(0)) ∈ V means that two entries correspond to
the fundamental representation V , and another to an arbitrary irreducible (Lk,λ)(0) with
dominant weight λ in the open Weyl alcove at positive integer level k (k ≥ 1 for g ̸= E8
and k ≥ 2 g = E8).
We assume that (Lk,λ)(0) is either (W1)(0), or (W2)(0). Then in this case ((W1)(0) ⊗
(W2)(0)) ⊗ (W3)(0) admits a unique inner product such that (W1)(0) ⊗ (W2)(0) has the invariant inner product under the action of g with a fixed normalization on the highest weight
vectors, and such that for any dominant weight γ that results from the decomposition into
irreducible under the action of g such that γ is in the open Weyl alcove, we similarly endow
(Lk,γ)(0) ⊗ (W3)(0) with the unique invariant inner product under the action of g. In this
way we have a well defined decomposition of ((W1)(0) ⊗ (W2)(0)) ⊗ (W3)(0) into orthogonal
irreducible subspaces, which is canonical for g ̸= E8. Selecting only the irreducible components of (Lk,γ)(0) ⊗ (W3)(0) with dominant weights in the open Weyl alcove, gives the
projection (F0)2,1 from ((W1)(0) ⊗ (W2)(0)) ⊗ (W3)(0)) to that addendum and an the inclusion (G0)2,1 from that addendum to ((W1)(0) ⊗ (W2)(0)) ⊗ (W3)(0)). Then apply Theorem
37.24 which implies the assumptions of a). Then we may proceed with the computations
in the proof of Prop. 38.14.

38.6. Identification between the Huang-Lepowsky braided symmetry for vertex operator algebra modules and Wassermann and Toledano Laredo braiding
operators.
Remark 38.16. We emphasize that while our analysis in Sections 37 and 38 focuses
primarily on the Huang–Lepowsky tensor product and the comparison of associativity
morphisms, the corresponding braided ribbon structure is completely determined and connected to the braided ribbon structure described in Section 35 in the setting of loop group
conformal net. Specifically, in the VOA setting the category-theoretic braiding operator
RW1,W2is defined via the analytic continuation and geometric rotation of vertex operator intertwining fields, satisfying the formula R(Y (v, z)) = e
zL(−1)Y (v, eπiz) [88]. For
the category of modules of affine Lie algebras at positive integer levels, the existence of
this braided vertex tensor category structure was established by Huang and Lepowsky
in [89]. The eigenvalues of the resulting double-braiding (monodromy) operator on an
irreducible component W3 ⊆ W1 ⊠ W2 are governed by the ribbon balancing relation
θW1⊠W2 = RW2,W1 ◦RW1,W2 ◦ (θW1 ⊗θW2), where the twist is given by the Virasoro exponent
θ = e
2πiL(0) by Theorem 4.1 in [93]. On the highest-weight spaces, this yields the explicit
monodromy eigenvalue λ = e
2πi(h3−h1−h2)
, where hi are the conformal weights of the respective VOA modules. Via the Sugawara construction hλ =
Cλ
2(k+h∨)
, these spectral phases
translate precisely into the quadratic Casimir expressions yielding the quantum group
parameter values ±q
1
2
(C3−C1−C2)
. This coincides exactly with the explicit monodromy formulas for the Knizhnik–Zamolodchikov equations obtained via the operator-algebraic loop
group framework of Toledano Laredo [169], which we detailed in Section 35, Corollary
35.1. This numerical and structural agreement completely reconciles the monodromy data
of both settings while bypassing the need for an independent geometric verification of the
braiding axioms.
